STOCKTON HEATH.

DIOCESAN SUNDAY.—The annual diocesan sermons were preached in St. Thomas's Church on Sunday, in the morning by the Rev. A. F. White, of Warrington, and in the evening by the Hon. and Rev. C. F. Cross, -vicar of Stretton. The collections amounted to £6 14s. 4d., which is an increase upon last year's amount. ANNIVERSARY SERVICES AT HILL CLIFFE.—The summer anniversary, which is the principal event of the year in connection with Hill Cliffe Baptist Church, was celebrated on Sunday. In the morning a flower service was held in the chapel, when the children having assembled in the school, each with a bouquet of flowers, walked in single file down the aisle and presented their floral offerings to Mrs. Hughes and Miss Moss, who placed them on the communion table. The service was -of exceptional interest. Miss Dutton, of Moore, had promised to give an address, and the audience was an exceptionally large one for a morning service, the chapel being full. The Pastor presided and read the lessons, the Rev. S. D. Thomas, of Earlestown, offering prayer. Master Stanley Jones, sang " Count your many blessings" in a pure sweet voice. Suitable hymns were sung. and Miss Dutton gave an addrees on the teaching and lessons of flowers. Mr. James Strange presided at the organ. In the afternoon the chapel was filled. The Pastor twain conducted the service, and the sermon was preached by the Rev. S. D. Thomas on " Righteous- ness." Miss Lucy Davies, of Hollinfare, feelingly sang "Come unto Me." A select choir under the leadership of Mr. J. M. Muir rendered the anthem " Hear my prayer" in excellent style, the singing of the united choir being good during the whole service. Mr. Muir presided at the organ. Tea was provided in the school- room for visitors. For the evening service the chapel was filled, and an overflow service was held in the large schoolroom. The service in the chapel was conducted entireFy by the Rev. S D. Thomas, who spoke upon the case of the young ruler who came to Christ. Miss Lucy Davies again delighted the congregation with a credit- able rendering of" Sun of my soul." The Chapel choir sang the anthem "Therefore with Angels" and well known hymns were contributed and joined most heartily in by the congregation. Mr. James Strange was at the organ. The overflow service in the schoolroom was conducted by the Pastor, who preached on " Galleo the indifferent." Miss Moikon`was at the (organ and the hearty, though brief service was appreciated. The collections for the day amounted to about £3O.

WHAT CAUSES RHEUMATISM. And Why Dr. Williams Pink Pills for Pale People Cure It.

Mr. Frederick Turner, of East-street, Bridgtown, Cannock, isa noted local musician, and a man of great intelligence. His narrative (confirmed by the "Walsall Observer" newspaper) of how Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People cured him of Rheumatic fever—the worst form of Rheumatism—is therefore worthy of special attention, esppcially as it shows what Rheumatism is, and h(

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Duchess of Teck who, with the Duke, represented their Majesties the King and Queen at the wedding of Princess Alexandra of Cumberlend, is a lady of great charm. She is considered to be very like her sister-in- law, the Princess of Wales, and as she dresses her hair in thq same style, this adds still further to the resem- blance. Her early married life was anything but a smooth one. Immediately after her wedding, her husband was ordered to the front, next, her father,the late Duke of Westminster died, and a little later her father-in-law, the Duke of Tack was laid low. Then came the great anxiety with regard to the sight of her first-born, but this trouble has happily almost passed away. The Duchess of Teck is devoted to her children, and rides exceptionally well. Women Officers.—A woman has to be a very exalted personage indeed to he given command of a regiment, and even then the appointment is usually purely an honorary one. Our Queen is chief of several regiments, the Duchess of Connaught is the nominal Commander of a regiment which was formerly captained by her illu- strious father, Prince Frederick Charles of Prussia, and the Duchess of Saxe-Coburg is head of a Regiment of Russian Dragoons, and Colonel of a German Grenadier Regiment. The Empress of Russia is Colonel-in-Chief of no fewer than four regiments, Queen Margberita of Italy is chief of abattalion of German mounted troops, while Queen Wilhelmina rules over a cavalry corps on her own account. Many other women" colonels" might be mentioned, some of whom lead their regiments occa- sionally on peaceful parades of State. Titled Authoresses.—Lady Cromartie has joined the numerous band of literary women in Society, and has written a little volume of Celtic tales entitled " The End of the Song." Lady Marjorie Gordon, whom Mr Gladstone described as "the cleverest child " he had ever met, was an adroit writer whilst in her teens, as was also the Duchess of Sutherland, who has written a book of travels and two or three novels. There are several Duchesses who figure as authoresses, including 1 the Duchess of Somerset. The Duchess in the book so often occupies a large place in modern fiction, that it seems only right that, after the manner of the Duchess of Sutherland, she should sometimes become a writer as well as an inspirer of romance. But Duchesses do not usually write about Duchesses, and the Duchess of Sutherland is particularly democratic in her style. But this only follows the general rule that the class among whom our daily lot is ca.st soon becomes uninteresting, and, irrespective of rank, one always seeks to escape from it when writing or reading for recreation. That is no doubt why the factory girl prefers to read stories of duchesses and belted earls, rather than of people of her own class. The Uncommon Virtues of Gratitude and Content- ment.—The Rev. .1. Wilson, president of the Baptist Union. recently gave an address on the subjects of gratitude and contentment. He referred to the writings of Burns, and said that while he had read many stories of the miserable poor, he considered there were more miserable people among the rich. There are compensa- tions in all the affairs of life, and no doubt this is per- fectly true. The poor and contented man is infinitely better off than he with riches, which can buy him nothing that he has not already, and who has no resources other than the monotonous round of a life of pleasure. And yet the world has seen very few rich men willing to take the poor man's burden, though there are countless numbers of poor people who would be glad to accept the rich man's lot. , Robbing the Necessitous.—Several young women seeking situations have written to London papers to complain that they have been charged fees by governess agencies. none of which has sent them a single address. ks a rule they have heard nothing further from the agencies, but one lady experienced an unpleasant ex- ception, being sent for to the office of the agency, in order that she might re-state her qualifications, the journey which she could ill afford to make at her own expense,being as resultless as if she had remained at home For govemesses there is a registration office at 47, Har- ley Street, Which is conducted on eleemosynary lines. and charges no fees, so that women of that profession need not incur any monetary risks, but one regrets to know that there are several people in London who trade upon the difficulty which people in some professions ex- perience in finding employment, and charge fees for which, as they know perfectly well, they will be unable to make any return. It is a cruel fratia,and it is regret- able that in so many cases it is carried on with im- punity. Of course the lace is selected with due regard to of the Bridgtown Cannock Bond, who was cured the toilette it is to adorn but lace of some description of terrible Rheumatism by Dr. Williams' Pink must appear, if we would be in" Fashions van." it impossible for him to see her until he knew mem And the girl carried this about in her heart for three days till the pain was like to break it. On the fourth the blow fell. A letter came from her lover, short, crushing, deadly. "Dearest, —I can still write dearest,' though all is, and must be, over between us. We must never meet again. I have deceived you as much as I have been deceived myself. Seven years ago, when little more than a lad, I was married—lured, cheated, fooled into it; but. married. My wife left me after a while, and I believed that she was dead Honestly believed it, Rezah, as I know you will believe; and then I asked you to marry me. Four days ago, to my horror, I had news indirectly of her; and now she has returned to claim me. My great pain in all this is to have wrecked your life, as I fear I have; my one consolation in it all, the knowledge that I have been loved by you. But we are parted for ever. Oh, my dearest! God bless you. —FRANK. " The girl met the blow like the brave soul that she was. All the morning she did her work at her office with regularity, and only a little absent- mindedness. In the afternoon she took a holiday, and went in search- of her old lover. She wanted to hear the truth from his lips, and to be absolutely certain that everything was as he said. But she could not find him. At his office they told her he had left; and one of the clerks, know- ing her, came out after her, and told her he thought be had gone abroad. She went straight to his rooms. There a very unpleasant experience awaited her. She was speaking to the landlady, who was telling her that he had certainly gone away, somewhere abroad—to the Colonies, she thought—when the wife appeared. She was a: flaunting, large-faced woman, gaudily dressed, and much older than Frank or Rezah. She was red in the face, too, and looked as if she had been drinking. "I know you," she said readily to Remit. "You're the girl Irith the queer name that that blessed 'usband of mine thought he was going to marry. But he's mine, don't you see? I've got 'im ; and, what's more, I mean to jolly well stick to 'im. See ? " and she shook her head and leered with shallow, boastful cunning. "I ain't going to be made miserable for nothing." Rezah looked at her steadily.

"I'm sorry your life has been an unhappy one," she said quietly. "But that is no reason why you should be glad that others are also as unhappy as yourself." And she turned on her heel. The little encounter did her good. It broke the monotony of her misery; and that night she reasoned the whole position out in her practical way. More than once the words she had spoken to her mother about Mr. Mitchell recurred to her, and gradually the idea of doing what had seemed so repugnant to her appeared less and less distasteful and difficult. The real reason was probably that she .longed for the excitement of some complete change from the usual life, and thus turned to the idea of marriage with a sense of relief. "Mother, what have you told Mr. Mitchell " she asked on the following day, much to her mother's surprise and annoyance, because Mrs. Warrender had put it off as a most unpleasant duty. "I haven't seen him, child. How could I tell him ? " She spoke angrily to give herself courage. "Do you very much wish what he wishes ? " Her mother looked up in keen, quick surprise. "You're looking ill, Rezall. is anything the matter?

_ "I have had a blow. Frank and I are parted— for good." Mrs. Warrender tried not to let her pleasure at the news overshadow her concern at the effect which she saw it had on Rezah. "What is the matter? lam sorry." But her voice was a struggle to express sympathy. The girl understood her well enough. "It's a little hard that a blow that seems as if it would break one's life in two should give more pleasure than anything else to the only soul on earth where one ought to look for consolation." She sighed, and after a moment added: "Never mind, mother. You can't help feeling relieved that after all you may get out of this scrape with Mr. Mitchell, and she bent and kissed her on the fore- head. "If this should ever be possible it will only be after Mr. Mitchell has had the whole truth told to him. And then, if he knows everything—mind, everything—and is willing to make me his wife, I shall be glad to marry him." "You are a dear, sweet, obedient girl, Rezah." The daughter smiled at this most unexpected comment, but said nothing. "I'll tell Mr. Mitchell," added Mrs. Warrender.

Then 'with a flash of great earnestness the girl said:

"One word, mother. You must tell him every- thing—that, my heart has been given to Frank; that I do not, and probably never shall, love him; that I come to him for rest; and that if it were not impossible to marry Frank I should do so. Mind, everything. And remember that I shall go over the whole ground again after you and before the marriage, and shall tell him all myself, so that nothing will be gained by holding back even the veriest trifle now."

"Do you think I would deceive you or him, in such a matter ? " asked the mother, with the anger of a person whose thoughts have been clearly read and her intentions anticipated. What was caid in the first interview with Mr. Mitchell was not told to the girl, but her mother said that he readily agreed to all; and Mrs. Warrender was so jubilant that Rezah was rather inclined to believe there was much more than she had been led to think at any time. But nothing could have been more kind and com- passionate and tactful than Mr. Mitchell's conduct. Ile was a spare, reserved, self-contained man of nearly forty. "Your mother has told me of your trouble, my dear," he said, taking her hand, when they met. '1 like Frank Mowbray, as everyone must like him who knows him. A s,traight, honourable, clever, fearless man; and I know without your telling me I hat whatever the cause may be that separates you, there will be nothing in it that touches his honour. I cannot truthfully tell you that I would rather see you his wife than mine. But if it cannot be that you marry me, I would rather you married him than anyone. ' In this way he let her see that he knew the whole story and he shewed her how he wished matters to stand as between them. She liked this, especially when she came to see how little it left for her to say about her own feelings. "Do you think thltt the feeling I can give to you will bring you happiness? It will be little more than friendship. No more, indeed," she said musingly. "I shall ask no more, if you will make me one promise—always to think of me as a tried and trusted friend, to be treated with absolute

confidence." "I will promise that," she replied after a pause of thought. "I have been a lonely man, Rezah, with little or no real interest in life strong enough to stir any innermost emotions—till this love of mine for you. And now—well, you cannot understand, and I cannot describe, all that this means. God give me strength to be a tower of help to you, and to win in some measure that second abiding love that can spring flora the ashes of a dead passion. lam content, dear." He lifted her fingers to his lira and kissed them. "And now let us talk as we have often talked together when you suspected nothing of my real feeling—like the friends we are going to be for life."

—She always remembered that interview with a sense of refreshing gratitude. And it pleased her too, greatly, that he said nothing directly about the marriage, and neither then nor at any time afterwards pressed her as to the time of the marriage. Instead of this, he drew her shrewdly to take a great interest in a number of projects and schemes that he-had in hand, or took in hand for the particular purpose. Thus the instinctive craving for change that had led her to contemplate the idea of marriage with him at first was satisfied, and a new set of interests were brought into her life in consequence. Mrs. Warrender was anxious to get the marriage fixed for as early a date as possible, and with this object spoke to Mr. Mitchell. But he would not hear of it. "No, no; not on any consideration. Let it. come naturally." "Yes, and perhaps Frank Mowbray malt come Faek first with some tale to • distimb Revili, and knock the whole thing on the head." "Ali, you don't understand your child, Mrs. Warrender. Nothing can ever knock the whole th,, in on the head, as you call it, With Rezah—at least: only one thing—' f that bar between them were removed." "Well, there's always danger in delays," said the widow petulantly. "My friend, lily engagement to Rezah has done two things. It has increased my love and admira- tion for ber a thousandfold ; and it has purified— if that is the word—my feeling for her till I can believe that even if young Mowbray were to come back able to marry her, I could even hring myself to be enoush her friend to find my Zapping's—.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

The coat and skirt shows no sign of becoin• Mg a less necessary item of our wardrobe than of yore, and this summer it is to be met with in an even greater variety of styles. These range from the severe tailor-made with a simply cut coat and plainly made skirt of a comfortable walking length to elaborate boleros and much trimmed skirts of trailing and be-flounced description. There is, of course, an intermediate variety which is made of voile or light cloth with a dressy little coal: finished by fanciful revers or ornate little capes or, perchance, made with one of the long yokes so much in vogue from which the sleeves spring in a series off gaugings and then pouch into fulness which is caught at the wri.st into a rather wide cuff. Indeed this form of coat and skirt has become a steadfast feature of fashionable dress and has much to recommend it, especially on account of its adaptability to .11 and every circumstance of social daily life.

There are some very pretty costumes of this style made in delicate coloured linens of a soft make, and also in pretty canvas-like materials which will make a very useful summer dress for warm days. Embroidered linen is, of course, the smartest material at present, but in a good quality is apt to prove rather costly, a pretty little blouse I saw the other day of embroidered linen being priced at twenty-five shillings. However, there are less fine quali- ties which are not so expensive, and although rather coarser in texture wear and look well. One of these latter forms the subject of my first sketch, and is made up in a particularly pretty style, although there is nothing elabo- rate about it. The edear of the linen is a soft pale green with the embroidery in pink and green. The loose sleeves are cut up in a novel style,while the long curve of the collar narrow- ing into the waist is becoming to any kind of figure. The embroidery alone is used for trimming the pretty gown, the only other ornamental feature of which is a soft kid belt fastened by an ornamental buckle. A pictur- esque hat of pale green chip trimmed by a long curling ostrich feather and a cluster of pink roses under the brim completes the exceed- ingly charming toilette. Ecru linen relieved by white embroidery is very modish, especially when completed by a gracefully curved black hat. Indeed, this combination of colours is a favourite one in the most fashionable gowns, whether of linen, voile, or silk mousseline.

INTY TOILETTE.

The dainty toilette depicted is a charming confection of net over pink silk trimmed with a handsome lace flounce and puffings of pink silk caught in with small brilliant buckles. The bodice comes low down on the arms, a strap of tiny pink roses crossing the shoulders. A silk fringe finishes the lower ,edge of the lace berthe and a chou of pink chiffon is arranged in the front of the decolletage. The skirt is made in the fashionable gauged style below the waist and is of the most up to date and approved description. There are many little social functions and small dances for which a toilette like this would look very effective, and it has the advantage of enabling one to use up an old gown for the foundation. In yellow it would look most striking with creamy yellow roses tinged with pink arranged on black velvet ribbon for the shoulder straps and the same flowers bunched in front. Net either plain or adorned with a small floral spray or tiny dots makes up into charming overdresses and can be got in a wide variety of colourings. The handsome lace flounce at the foot of this skirt is a pleasing change from the much favoured series of tiny frills or flouncings which, al- though exceedingly pretty, have been the vogue for several seasons and have therefore outlived their novelty. A PRETTY HAT.

realms of dress is ever for a fresh mode or an original departure for the wheel of is fashion apt to revolve somewhat monotonously. Mil- linery is at its best at present and a very pretty but simple hat is given in my last sketch. It curves down over the face in front in the approved fashion of this season, and is trimmed with bunches of shaded parma violets with a lace fall at the back. Flowers and lace separately employed or used in con- junction appear on every description of hat and toque, while the fanciful straws of all sorts are used for the formation of the shape itself. Lace, indeed, in many instances is used so liberally that one is almost constrained to believe that the hat itself is made entirely of it, with only a wreath of roses or some delicate blossoms of most natural-looking appearance visible at intervals under the folds. A charm- ing hat I saw the. other day was draped with Spanish lace and had a stiff wreath of pink roses arranged round the crown. It was bent down at the back and shaded the face of the wearer in front. Indeed, one seldom or ever sees the hat of last year which was turned back from the face in front, and these are now quite out of fashion, gem Cogintnal

REZAH.

BY EVELYN SWAN,

Author of " 'Twixt Love and Fear," "The Snare of Gold," "A Penitent's Revenge," "Little Sally's Luck," &c.

"No, mother, I cannot. I would do almost Anythinc,, for you and the others, but this I cannot." •

"I can't see why you're so unreasonable, Rezah. Your commonsense, that you're so fond of. talking about., might at least shew you the advantages of acting in a common-sense way now. You'll be sorry enough when you're my age, and have children of your own. You'll understand then the difference between money and no money." "I understand it now, mother, well enough," said the girl, a tall, dark, clever-looking, clear- eyed girl, with an air of great self-reliance about her. "I don't grumble at having to work for my living; on the contrary, I like it, and wouldn't do otherwise if I had the chance; but I know also how hard the work sometimes is, sweet though the pay-days are when they come round. I love working for you, mamsie—but I can't sell myself, dear." She kissed her mother's worn, troubled, fretful, worried face. "You look very tired." "You'll look tired if ever you have five children all wanting to be fed, and clothed, and educated, and little or no money to do it all with. I can't think how I manage as it is. It's well for you that your work takes you out of the house all the day, and out of the fret and worry of it all at the same time." "Isn't that a little hard, mother ? "

"Well, I can't see for the life of me why you won't marry Mr. Mitchell," answered the mother, with more relevance than the words appeared to imply. Rezah's ref,iss.l to do this was, indeed, the cause of all Mrs. Warren:l.:Fs irritation.

"I think you can, dear, ;f vou try to think. There is only one reason. 1 Gen t love him, and I do love Frank. I should hate myself if I married one man when loving another." She spoke with a momentary air of proud self-confidence; and then, a moment later, a light of mischief and a laugh came into her eyes, cud, catching her mother round the waist, she said: "Why, mother, I declare positively that if Frank were as rich as he is poor I should still love him and marry him just the same."

But Mrs. Warrender was only irritated by the little jest, and moved away.

"I wish you'd be serious, Itezah," she said petulantly.

"As serious as my most tragic name, eh? If 1. were going to marry old Mr. Mitchell, I'm afraid I should be serious enough. But this evening, and now, I can't be serious, mamsie ; for Frank's coming for me, and we're going for a walk; and I declare I haven't seen him for a whole three days." "Well, I hope you'll never have cause to be sorry you've decided as you have." "I should probably be very sorry if I decided any other way. Ugh! what would life be as Mrs. Mitchell, rich, demure and cold? I should have to grow fat, flabby, and :colourless," and the girl made a gesture suggesting impossibility and repugnance.

"He is one of the kindest men on earth, child. There's nothing in his power to give you that he'd refuse."

"Why, mother dearest, what we women want most is to be denied things. If I thought Frank never meant to deny me anything, I should—oh, it would be horrid! Of course, he mustn't say he means to; but I must know it, all the same. If life were all just ask and get, there'd be nothing to live for."

"You're the most contrary girl on earth, Rezah." "And you're such a tyrannical, hard-hearted, brute-force mother that if I married Mr. Mitchell to please you, and then came to you at the end of half a year and said: Mother, this is a mistake,' you'd be fit to cry your eyes out to think you'd had a hand in making the match," and she smiled lovingly into her mother's face and kissed her again. "I want nothing but your happiness, child," said the mother, giving way. "Happiness doesn't lie that way, mamsie. I want something more in my life than the sober colours of gentleness .and krndness. I want the warm, glowing tints of love—my Frank's love. I'll take the storm for the .sakt:of the after-calm; but all calm would be intolerable. I'll tell you this, however. If I wanted to live a life of nothing but good works; if I sought a shelter in the arms of a dear, kind, staunch friend; if I were done with the battle of life and sought only rest and relief— I would choose no life more readily than to be Mr. Mitchell's wife. But as it is I'm going to marry Frank." And she kissed her mother, and was running out of the room when the latter called her back.

"Before you go, Rezah, there is something you ought to know. Her voice had a husk of nervous- ness in it. "You don't know all Mr. Mitchell's kindness. I—l am in—l mean, I have had to borrow money from him, and I owe him more than I can ever pay. I have lost a lot of money lately. 1. couldn't help it." She began to speak with hurried and rather unsteady tones. "You don't know what the expenses are with all the children. There are so many things to pay that I can't pay them all out of the small amount of money your father left me. I was obliged to have money from somewhere, and there was no one else to whom I could go but dear Mr. Mitchell." "Well, mother dear, I suppose you told him the condition of things, and he was able to estimate for himself the chances of getting it back again? If he was so kind as to lend it, he will not be so unkind as not to wait for the repayment till he can get it." "You don't understand these things, Rezah," replied her mother testily. She had something much more unpleasant to say yet, and she was afraid of what her daughter would think. "You think everything must be going all right just because you don't se it going wrong. You don't suppose, do you, that anyone would lend me money for the mere asking? That's not the world, I can tell you. Of course, I knew what Mr. Mitchell wanted about you—"

"Stop, mother," cried the girl sharply, as one in pain. "Don't, please, tell me that you stooped to borrow money from Mr. Mitchell by leading him to think that there could possibly be any chance of his marrying me. You don't really mean that, do you ? " She caught her mother by the hands, and looked eagerly into her eyes: She read there the confirmation of her fears. "Have you taken any of this money since I've been engaged to Frank ? ' "I don't know what the world's coming to when girls tale to judging their parents in this way. One would suppose you were mother and I daughter, Reza."

"Our. positions are a little reversed, miller, I'm afraid. It's you who've done wrong, and I who am ashamed. Of course,- Mr. Mitchell mnst be told at once; and if you've not told him by the next time I see him, I shall tell him. You'd bettor write." And without saying any more, but feeling bitterly humiliated, the girl went out of the morn. She hated everything that was underhand. she burnt 'it the idea that she should have been made the subject of such a bargain. And the smart of the thing was all the worse because she felt sure that it was not Mr. Mitchell, but her mother, who had made the terms. It galled her to come in contact in this sharp, rugged manner with what she knew to be her mother's failings. But she could not excuse her in this; and she feared, moreover, that even now she did not know all the truth.

She was right enough in this. As soon as she was alone Mrs. Warrender sat down, and, taicirm a letter from her pocket, read it twice through again, though she knew the Contents almost by heart already ; and she puckered her forehead iu sheer perplexity how to answer it. It was from Mr. Mitchell, asking whether sha thought he might yet propose to Rezah, and reminding her of the many things she had men- tioned of the girl's inclination towards him. What. to say she could not think. She had, in truth, completely deceived him, and she was now over- whelmed with the fear that when he found out the truth he would make her repay him everything at once. It was characteristic of her that she put all the blame for the trouble on her daughter. That was her first comfort; her next was to put off the writing of any letter until the following day. Meanwhile Rezah was sorely troubled on her own account, and went about with so anxious a look that her mother, setting it all down to the debit of her own act, was inclined to be angry with the girl for making so much of it.. Rezah's trouble was of the heart, however. Her lover had not met her as he had promised; but instead had sent a telegram vaguely worded, and hinting at coming mischief, and afterwards had written saying that something had come to his knowledge, something affecting him only, that made

===== 0002601_19040625_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
mournful, and different, of course, bu still happiness—in seeing her happy as his wife." Mrs Warrender looked at hini out of the corners of her eyes, and sniffed with an indescribable suggestion that she had no words sufficiently eloquent to express her opinion of his quixotic foolWiness.

me man was rignt enough as to the fixing of the date of the wedding. Rezah herself mooted the subject to her mother, and in this way the time was settled ; and the girl took the keenest pleasure in arranging that everything should be in just that form and style which she thought would best pleas! her husband that was to be.

It was a perfectly quiet wedding; and though the children wished to make the most of the oppor- tunity for a lot of fun, it was in their view a very tame affair, and dull.

Mrs. Warrender .was very thankful and self- congrstulatory when it was all over, and the two had driven away for their honeymoon, which was to be a very long one. It was all her own tact and diplomacy, she thought, that had managed it all ; and her sole tiought about her daughter was that liezah ough: m be entirely grateful to her for having 'fowl( :ter a husband who had so much money. That, he was a man of exceptional parts was reckoned by her as of no account. He was a "good match," and she had made it.

-One circumstance only was there to cause her even a momentary twinge. A letter had come for Rezah on the very morning of the wedding; and Mrs. Warrender, knowing the writing, and that it came from "that young Mowbray," had purposely "forgotten " 'to give it to her daughter. At bottom, Nile was a good deal afraid of Rezah; and although it had seemed a somewhat small thing to keep it Lack or forget it in the hurry of the preparations, now that she thought it over "in cold blood" she saw that there might easily be a good deal of unpleasantness. She would have liked to destroy it, but dared not ; so she posted it to the first place where the couple were to stop, with a line of apology for her " forget fulness. " All unsuspecting the tragedy that lay within the envelope for her, Rezah opened it, with the simple remark that there was a "letter from mother."

When she saw the writing on the enclosure she turned pale. She knew it well enough. Thoughtful for her husband, however, she put restraint on herself, and waited to read it until she was alone in her room.

It told her almost at a glance that Frank Mow- bray had now good reason to believe that in a short, time he would be free again that he had discovered certain things about the wife which would probably divorce the tie that had become so hateful to him ; and that if Rezah could wait with a little patience things might yet be as they had been for them both. As she read everything seemed to grow dark and cold; and the girl sat and shivered, and breathed hard, and interlaced her 4ngers, and pressed them until they were hurt.

Her thoughts were a complete blank. Then an old favourite quotation came jumbling into her head, bringing a word picture. "Love took up the glass-of Time and turned it in his glowing hands," and the mental picture changed to Love staring, pained and frightened, at the broken hourglass, let slip from his nerveless lingers. "Yes ; it is broken," she murmured to herself as she rose, with one deep, heavy sigh. Till then, she had not seen the date of the letter. When she did so, came the perception of her mother's treaclury ; and the anger against the latter was a relief from the pain. "Rezah, are you coming out ? " said her husband, entering the room at this point. "In a minute or two, dear," she answered, forcing a smile, which he did not see was unreal. "See, I have had a mishap with a letter. I have a lette; from Frank Mowbray, which ought to have reached me yesterday or the day before. He tells toe that he is likely to get a divorce from his wife." She held it out to him.

He looked at her fixedly for fully a minute before speaking. "Poor chil(, he murmured, then, in a voice so toned with sympathy and compassion that its genuine sorrow was unmistakable: "What can we do? " The question was no more than a whisper of helplessness. Then he sat down like one dazed. He was infinitely more overcome than she. And he buried his face in one hand.

The letter had fluttered to the ground and lay Letween them.

"I would give all I'm worth to undo what we did yesterday," he said at length. "You must try and think if I can do anything to make it easy for )ou." Then she knelt down by him, and it was rather as if the sorrow were his and she the comforter.

"See, John," she said, after a while, taking up the letter. "We have no secrets, you and I; and we'll have no half-confidences, nor, if we can help it, thoughts of the might-hate-been. See," and kbe tore the letter in little pieces. "There, that is all Cr, never even to be thought of except as between tmo friends with no secrets, husband. No secrets."

She kissed him affectionately twice, gave a little s gh, and then rising, said, in a cheery tone and anner :

"And now we'll start for our walk—the walk, a- d, that is to last all through life." nd when he looked at her she saw that his eyes e!t hick wit!i tear..

iz EZ:P. - • PRETTY WEDDING AT HELSBY. Bennett—Gornall.

There was a pretty weVlding at Helsby on Saturday when Ethel,eldest daughter of the late Mr.J.H. Gornall, J. P. Medical Officer of Health for Warrington, and Mrs. Gornall was married to Mr. Joseph Bennett, of Doncaster. A larize congregation assembled in the picturesquely-situated church to witness the ceremony. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. H. K. Gornall, brother of the bride, assisted by the Rev. E.W. Evans, vicar of St. Paul's who also gave an address. The bride was given away by her uncle, Mr. E. J. Guest, J. P., of Oak Mount,Helsby. She was becomingly dressed in cream silk,trimmed with chiffon and lace frilis,and wore a wreath of orange blossom and myrtle, Honiton lace veil, and carried a bouquet of white roses and lilies of the valley. The bridesmaids were Miss Mary Gornall, sister of the bride, and Miss Elsie Guest, her cousin. They wore pretty dresses of pale blue eollienne' trimmed with cream lace, with hats to match, and had bouquets of, pink sweet peas. The best man was Mr. J. W. Bennett brother of the bridegroom. Master Lawrence Guest Gornall, son of Dr. John Guest Gornall,Medical Officer of Health for Warrington,acted as page and was dressed in cream serge. The organist was Mr. Arthur Down, of Stockton Heath, cousin of the bride, who played the Bridal March from " Lohengrin." as the bride was led to the chancel. He also played Mendelssohn's "Wedding March" after the ceremony. The hymn sung by the lcongregation was "The voice that breathed o'er Eden." Zany valuable presents numbering about 100 were not on view as the marriage took place from Oak Mount IHelsby, where a large number of the friends of the I bride and bridegroom assembled after the ceremony. The happy pair left the house amid a shower of confetti on their way to the Lake District where they will spend the honeymoon. The bride's travelling dress consisted of dark blue face cloth, trimmed with coffee coloured Ilace and silk, and hyacinth toque.

LEFT HER SICK CHILD TO ITS FATE.

At Widnes on Monday, Mary Jordan, 52, Nelson-st., was charged with neglecting her child, Agnes, aged five months. On May 14, it was alleged, the women loft home after a few words with her husband and did not return until Thursday last. At the time she left the child was dangerously ill, and eventually died on May 23. The Mayor said the woman had behaved most unnaturally. The superintendent of police had given her a good character apart from this, and she would be bound over for six months in two sureties of .25. REVIVAL OF WITCHCRAFT IN EAST ANGLIA. Dr. C. B. EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

TEE CORDON-BENNETT RACE.

The Motor Derby, as the Gordon Bennett Cup race has grown to be called familiarly, has been run with great success at Homburg. A large gathering from all over Europe, .collected under the personal aegis of the Raiser himself, who took the keenest interest 431 everything from start to finish, and not Only was the race run faster than ever before, but it was marred by no accidents or tragedies, such as have spoilt other great motor contests. Yet it is being asked on ,every side whether this should not be the last contest, whether the awful risks involved •and the money wasted is worth it, for there IS little doubt that it is more by good luck than by good judgment that the race was %free of accidents and tragedies. The English Automobile Club for one is in favour of abolishing the race, and the subject is certain in the near future to come up for discussion. 111 this latest race there is no doubt that the right car won in M. school to the

Oddington was from Weston-super-Mare; and it was a Somerset County Council scholarship which laid the foundation of his final success, enabling him to go to Owen's College, Manchester. Born on December 28th, 1882, the Senior Wrang- ler is only twenty-one, yet he is a B.Sc. both of London and Victoria Universities, having held at the latter the University Scholarship for Physics. Two years ago he won a scholarship at Trinity College, Cambridge, from Owen's College, and since then he has swept t'he board, taking the Mathematical Tripos at the end of his second year instead of the third, which is a most unvnual thing, showing great self-confidence and exceptional ability. AMERICAN STEAMBOAT DISASTER.

The disaster to the American pleasure Steamer, General Slocum, is one of the most appalling within the memory of man, and resulted in the loss of close on a thousand lives. It took place near Hell Gate on the East River, within sight of New York, awl the fact that the majority of the victims were women or children renders it all the mosi distressing: The occasion was the annual outing of the Sunday School of the Lutheran Church of St. Mark's, and -a very large party was proceeding by steamer to Locust Grove, a pleasure resort on Long Island Sounds Flags were flying and bands were playing, when suddenly off Sunken Meadows fire broke out in the luncheon saloon and spread with terribie rapidity, making such swift progress as quickly to become uncontrollable, The General Slocum was hemmed in by the Hell Gate rocks and could not turn, so the captain put on full speed ahead, beaching her on North Brothers Island. Meanwhile, an ar

palling panic had ensued, many people jump ing overboard to escape burning, and .throw- ing their children into the sea. The lifebelts proved out of date and useless, and only - heightened the horrors by catching fire and blazing up. As the steamer made for the shore full steam ahead, she left in her track a train of struggling forms in the water. Tugs and other small vessels hastened to their assist- ance and saved many lives: but the crowning horror of all was when the main deck gave way and precipitated its human burdeninto the raging flames below. The disaster proved the opportunity for many deeds of heroism : but the most intense indignation has been aroused throughout the States over. certain points in the condition and administration of the General Slocum, and a most searching inquiry into all the surround- ing circumstances is to be held.

HR. PHILIP STANHOPE RETURNS TO PARLIAMENT.

The voting in the Market Harborough Division of Leicestershire for the election of a Parliamentary representative to fill the vacancy caused by the resignation of Mr. J. W. Logan (Liberal) has resulted in the return of the Hon. Philip Stanhope, the Egures reading, Hon. P. Stan-

hope (c), 7,843.

PhilipStanhope, is much better known than his opponent. the

fOivrth and youngest son of Phillip Henry, fifth Earl Stan- hope, the emi-

nent historian,

, and a former Under-Secre-

tary for Foreign HON. PHILIP H.STANIIOI3E,m.P. Affairs and

Secretary to the defunct Board of Control, he is a very advanced Reformer, and chair- man of the National Reform Union, and takes a keen interest in historical portraiture which led to his appointment some few years since as a trustee of the National Portrait Gallery. In 1877 he married Alex- andra, daughter of the late Count Cancria- land, widow of Count Tolstoi. Mr. Stanhope has had a varied electoral experience. In 1880 he unsuccessfully contested Dover, and in 1885 Wednesbury, when he was de- feated by Mr. W. Lloyd. This gentleman was defeated by Mr. Stanhope at the ensuing General Election, of 1886, who in his turn defeated Mr. Stanhope at the General Elec- tion of 1892. At a by-election for Burnley ia February, 1893, Mr. Stanhope . was returned for the borough by a majority of 693, which fell in 1895 to 321, owing the intru- sion of a Socialist Candidate, and in 1900 was wiped out altogether, the present mew her, Mr. William Mitchell, polling 6,774. to Stanhope's 6,173.

The theory is advanced and generally accepted that Backache is a sure symptom of Kidney Disease, and that as Kidney Disease develops into HO many serious and fatal forms such as Bright's Disease, Diabetes, Dropsy, Rheumatism, &c., the backache must be cured at once else the victim may answer for it with his or her life. WATER POLO.

Wigan and St. Helens met in the Liverpool and Dis- trict League at the Wigan Corporation Baths on Wed- nesday night. St. Helens were without Lawrenson, his place being taken by Manning. Wigan, who first defended the shallow end,were strongly represented,and early on showed splendid form. Saunders twice defeated Turner with brilliant shots, whilst Hodgson scored a third goal. Manning scored for the visitors soon after the interval, and Saunders scored Wigan's fourth goal. Siiarrock added one for the visitors. Result:—Wigan 4, St. Helens 2.—ln a second.division encounter Wigan Reserves beat St. Helens Reserves by 8 to 1.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE EDUCATION COMMITTEE. Taking Children From School to Church.

At a meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee held at Crewe on Monday, Dr. Hopkinson, Vice. Chancellor of the Victoria University of Manchester, and Dr. Dale, Vice-Chancellor of the Liverpool University, with several other gentlemen, attended as a deputation to lay before the committee the position of the Universities with regard to education in Cheshire, and asked for an increased gent to assist those Univer- sities. After hearing the views of the deputatior, the Chairman said the Committee of Education for Cheshire desired to co-ordinate their work with that of the Universities as ranch as possible, but their financial resources were very limited. He promised, however, that the subject should be carefully considered. The question was referred to the Higher Education Com- mittee.

On the subject of taking children from school to church, the Clerk read a letter from the Board of Education, which stated that, after careful consideration, the board decided that managers cannot direct teachers to take children to church, either for service or religious instruction during school hours.

IS REST NECESSARY?

Altrincham Mother's Views.

Notions to which even well-informed men have sub- scribed are being continually added to the long list of admitted mistakes of the past. Preachers agauast the bustle of modern life have never tired of pointing out, as a conclusive argument, that even machinery wears better for frequent rests, and now comes the newer idea that, as a matter of fact, machinery lasts longest and does the best work when kept up to its fullest speed.

-SS NELLIE WESTON. (Froia a photograph by _4. E. Martin, Altrincham..) It is to be hoped that this discovery will not lead to the suggestion that human beings should always live at the highest possible pressure. Unfortunately we have but two much proof of the terrible havoc which want of rest plays with the system. A "Cheshire County News" ireporter recently heard of a peculiarly striking case I which invited investigation. Mrs. H. Weston, of 10, Mill Street, Altrincham, supplied the facts willingly. My daughter Nellie, who is ten years old," Mrs. Weston began," had a bad seizure of St. Vitus' dance, which—it seems amazing to say it, but it is true—was 'completely cured by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pige people. Nellie was very poorly, and began twitching her hands and legs, and also falling off her food. This • latter surprised me, as she had always been a healthy • girl with a good appetite." The little one's I mother proceeded to relate the distressing I symptoms of this malady—the irregular and involuntary movements of the head and limbs, the languor which ' robs life of its pleasure, the convulsive clutching at things, and rolling about until, from ceaseless restless- ' ness and-complete exhaustion, the sufferer wasted away before her agonised mother's eyes. "When she was very ill indeed," continued Mrs. Weston, "I bought her two boxes of Dr. Williams' pink pills, and before she had taken one and a half boxes she was completely • better."

The happy motber added that the child was now in Ithe best of health, and a " big,fat, rosy girl." Asked how she came to hear of the wonderful cura- tive properties of Dr. Williams' pink pills, she said that an article in a newspaper caught her attention, and she "thought she would give them a trial." She" never tried anything else afterwards," was her additional remark. Mrs. Westoa contributed particulars of a similarly remarkable cure in the case of her eldest daughter. "Four years ago, she was also very ill with anfemia. I rsolved to try Dr. Williams' pink pills. My eldest girl was never strong. but I am glad to say she hss not had another illness since taking them. A doctor attended her," Mrs. Weston explained, " but seemed only to keep patching her up,' and every now and then she would break down again." "Yes," Mrs. Weston added, "they are undoubtedly the best pills to-day in either England, Sc•tland or Wales. My eldest daughter only took eight boxes of the pills in all. Nellie, you know took just 1, and whenever the former feels the least unwell she immediately gets Dr. Williams' pink pills, and Ands thorough relief."

Farm Fire in Cheshire.

On Wednesday afternoon, a fire originated mysteri- ously in the stockyard of Mr. Robert Williamson's farm, Ivy Bank, Crowton, four miles from Northwich, a stack of 50 tons, roughly estimated at £2OO, being ignited. The Northwich, Brigade fought the flames splendidly till a late hoer, and effectively prevented the fire fromteacbing five other stacks, which occu- pied the yard. The fifty tons were virtually ruined by fire and water. The iiay was a solid old stack, so that spontaneous combustion is out of the question. The loss is covered by insurance.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
THE P.S.A. MOVEMENT. Annual Meeting of the Wycliffe Society. Last Sunday's meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society was devoted to the transaction of the annual business connected with the society. A number of reports were read, and the annual election of officers took place. The President (Rev. John Yonge) presided. About 400 persons were present.

THE REPORTS.

Mr. B. FARR-tit (general secretary) submitted the eleventh annual report. All the meetings, he said, had bean marked with religious fervour and good feeling The aim of the brotherhood was to cultivate a' s pint of Christian brotherhood, to cheer, to elevate and to make the daily life Christianlike, to exert a healthy influence upon the life of the town and to lend a helping hand to those in need. There was no reason why the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society should not be made a greater influence for good. Every mem- ber could help to cheer the home, to purify the daily speech and to raise the morals of our municipal and political life. The work of absentee visitation had been well done, and let them never forget that where most attention was paid to visitation, there was the most prosperous P.S.A. The helping hand fund was also a very necessary fund in conveying practical com- fort in time of need. The sick club was still doing its helpful work with 56 members. Last year a bonus of 4s. was shared am ongst the members. The savings club was also doing well, although the number of depositors was not as large as the previous year, due probably to the depression in trade. There was now a temperance committee in connection with the brotherhood.

I ook-out or street committee would be a great help to the society. The prizes obtained this year numbered 743, or 37 more than the two preceding years, making a total of prizes distributed since the formation of the society of 9,989, or an average of 908 each year of the iociety's existence. The year closed with a member- ship of 560 and an average attendance of 370, about the same as the previous year. The new members enrolled this year was 466—an increase upon the number enrolled a year ago. The secretary proceeded to refer to the loss suffered by the society through deaths, and spoke of the indebtedness of the society to the officers and workers in connection with it. Let them not be discouraged he continued, because their numbers were not as large as they would like to see. Many men's meetings on Sunday afternoon had been started since the Wycliffe Society was inaugurated. There was plenty of room for all. There were move men outside the churches than there were inside the churches. If all the forces at work to reach the masses of workin,rbinen who did not attend a place of worship, would work in a Christ- like spirit and in brotherly love,indifference to religious observance would be largely overcome. Mr. R. ATILtNSON, the hon. treasurer, submitted the annual financial statement. The year commenced with a small debit balance but closed with a balance in hand of 12s. 10Ld. The receiptsamounted to £l3O 18s. 4d. Mr. R. Mit.Ls presented the absentee visitors' report. From this it appeared that 518 visitations had been paid during the year. Mr. JOSEPH OWES made a statement with reference to the benevolent fund. The amount distributed !in re- lief was £3 19s. Since the year closed a further sum of £2 12s. had been distributed.

Mr. DONALDSON, the secretary of the sick club said the club was inn satisfactory and prosperous condition, There were 56 members and at the end of the 'first quarter there was a 'sum of £lO 12s. 63d. in hand. After meeting all liabilities last year, there was a dividend of 4s. to full members and 2s. to half members. A sum of £6 3s.2Ld. was carried forward to the ensuing year. Mr. JAMES J. EVANI reported on the Savings Club There were 85 members, he said, and the amount de- posited was Xl7B 14s. 9d. The annual distribution would take place on the following Saturday. It was hoped to enrol more members next year. The CRAIRAI AN, in moving the adoption of the re- ports,said they weresatisfactory on the whole. In com- menting upon the attendance at the meetings he said there was a large number of men outside the churches. The P.S.A. was a workingman's institu- tion and he (Mr. Yonge) desired each member to use his best endeavours to bring others to the meetings. They were all very glad to hear that the treasurer of the society was able to finish the year with a balance in hand. The work in connection with the various organi- sations had been well done. He would like to see the Sick Club and Savings Club more popular. Warrington was a difficult town to move, but in some Lancashire towns these clubs were very useful adjuncts of P.S.A. societies and did much good work. The reports were adopted.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

MR. SAM WOODS.

Mr. S. 'Woods, the secretary of the Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Union Congress, about whose health it was stated last week his friends were showing great anxiety, is now as well as he has been at any time during the past 18 months. He is at the present time in London attending to Trade Union Congress business. The statement that he has been relieved of any of his work in connection with the Miners' Federatioa is quite erroneous. As EARLESTOWN SUICIDE.

On Wednesday afternoon Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest at Earlestown on George Dyer, who was drowned in the St. Helens and Sankey Canal on Mon- day morning. Dyer wasa sugar works labourer,and left a note on the kitchen table at the house of his uncle, Joseph Millington, Sankey Cottages, where he lived, stating that" this is the last time you will see me alive. Kiss little Jack for me, and my watch is for him." The jury returned a verdict of " Suicide whilst temporarily insane."

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

No CARNEGIE SUBSIDY FOR RUNCORN.

Runcorn Councilha-v-e'iii;Tio.if:a-w a grant from Mr. Carnegie towards the extension of their free public library. A number of communications have passed, the Scoto-American millionaire desiring details of manage- ment and finance. He has now replied that the income of the Library Committee would not support larger premises than at present exist. BOOKMAKER'S WANDERINGS.

Edward Broder, until recently oyster dealer and prosperous bookmaker, was charged at Runcorn, on Saturday, with wandering abroad in the early hours of the morning without visible means of subsistence. He has been charged with sleeping out twice within the past three weeks, and discharged. He was now sentenced to fourteen days' hard labour. BEGGING FROM A CoNsTABLE.—At Runcorn on Tues- day, James Thompson, who described himself as a Sheffield ironworker searching for work was charged with begging and with assaulting Police Constable Brayford and Mr. Walter Gregory, of Wellington-street. The prisoner stopped Brayford,who was in plain clothes and asked him for a penny for a shave. Brayford arrested hiar, and a violent struggle ensued. Brayford was kicked, and Mr. Gregory had his watch smashed. The prisoner was with great difficulty taken to the police station. He was sentenced to five weeks' hard labour. The bench thanked Mr. Gregory for the aid he had rendered.

ST. HELENS:

ALLEGED HIGHW AY ROBBERY.-ST. HELENS COLLIER BADLY MA CUED.

Thomas Gorry, of 78, Bold-street, was remanded by the St. Helens magistrates on Saturday on a charge of highway robbery with violence. IChief-inspector Goodall said that the charge was one of stealing 11. s. 9d. from the person of Peter Leach, collier, of 88, Talbot-street, and also a silk handkerchief, pipe and tobacco. &c. Leach and a friend were in Liverpool-road on Friday night, when they went into a chip-potato shop, and were followed by Gorry, the prisoner, and to com- panions. Gorry asked Leach to treat them •to chips and he did. They went out of the shop to the corner of Peter street. Leach gave them a pint bottle of beer that he had in his pocket, and they drank it. One of the men said that he knew where they could get some more beer, and Leach said he would pay for it if he would show him where to go. They then went on to Greenbank, and when' near the bottom of Copperas- street Leach was set on by Gorry and the others, and his money and hpndkerchief taken. He was left almost unconscious, and had great difficulty in getting to the Town Hall. The other men had not been arrested. When arrested by Police-constable Charlton Gorry answered" Not guilty." THE NEW 'VICAR OF ST. HELENS.

The Rev. Cyril C. B. iia—r-cisley, the new vicar of St. Helens, preached at the Parish Church on Sunday to large congregations. At the morning service he said he had at first thought it would be right to wait until his institution—that solemn service which was an appropri- ate commencement to a new ministry—before he addressed them, but he felt that it was right to come amongst them as arranged that day, because he knew there had been a true introduction and a mutual inter- cession. He asked very earnestly for their help not only on his own behalf, but on behalf of those who had already worked amongst them, and those who had come with him from Nottingham. In the afternoon there was an attendance of 700 men at the Bible class, when Mr. Etardsley delivered an impressive address. He hoped they would try to know one another as soon as pos- sible. It would be comparatively easy for the members of the class to know him, but not so easy for him to know them. He hoped they would regard one another as brothers, and the feeling that they regarded their !vicar as a brother man would stimulate, aad strengthen and cheer him.

The World Is Cuticura's Field, Used Wherever Civilization Has Penetrated.

I have suffered for some time from an excess of uric acid in the blood; and since the middle of last year, from a severe attack of Eczema, chiefly on the scalp, face, ears and neck and on one limb. I was for several months under professional treatments, but the remedies prescribed were of no avail, and I was gradually becoming worse, my face was dread- fully disfigured and I lost nearly all my hair. At last, my wife prevailed upon me to try the Cuticura Remedies and I gave them a thorough trial with the most satisfactory results. The disease soon began to disappear and my hair commenced to grow again. A fresh growth of hair is covering my head, and my limb (although not yet quite cured) is gradually improving. My wife thinks so highly of your remedies that she has been purchasing them in order to make presents to other persons suf- fering from similar complaints,, and, as President of the Bible Women's Society, has told the Bible women to report if any case should come under their notice when a poor person is so afflicted, so that your remedies may be resorted to."

LADY CYCLIST KILLED.

Miss Jane Jack (24), residing at Fowlis, was cycling down Gourdie Brae, near Dundee, when she lost control of the machine and crashed into a stone wall at a curve with great force. She was shockingly injured, and death supervened shortly after she was picked up.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
DIED WHILE PLAYING TENNIS.

A painful sensation has been caused in Longton by the death of Mr. Josiah Warner, a well known china manufacturer. Mr. Warner, who was 50 years of age, was playing a game of tennis at his residence on Tues- day evening, when he was seized with heart failure. He died in a couple of minutes.

THE EXAMINEE.

JUNE 25 1904.

Mr. BENN'S striking THE UNHAPPY majority has made MINISTRY. the Ministerial jour- nals very unhappy. The "Daily Telegraph" in a great rage says the Radical electors of Davonport belong naturally to the "predatory party." Why the "predatory party" should vote for Free Trade is a mystery We should have thought that Protection was more attractive to people in quest of plunder. The "Standard "sees in the Devonport election only too faithful an illustration of the fate which com- pels a Unionist candidate to slip into the meshes of Birmingham. "Usually," says our London contemporary "he confines himself to the lines laid down by Mr. BALFOUR, at Sheffield, for which there is something to be said; but he scarcely ever fails to hint, if not to declare openly, that he leans towards the idea of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. Bye-election after bye-election has shown conclusively that • these tactics are thoroughly distasteful to the general body of electors." This indicates a pretty clear perception of the fa its. Unfortunately the "Standard "is itself on slippery ground. It is not much use to commend the lines laid down by Mr. BALFOUR at Sheffield, and then to complain that Unionist candidates slide along them in the direction of Birmingham. That is what the Sheffield engineer clearly wished his candidates to do if they could manage it without alarming the general body of electors. They cannot manage it, but this is no reason for maintaining that something can be said for Mr. BALFOUR'S permanent way. The latest by3-election cannot be very soothing to Ministers, who are wrangling among themselves over army reforms. Mr. ARNOLD FORSTER IS believed to favour the adoption of the Esher scheme with little or no reservation. Lord LANSDOWNE and Mr. BRODRICK, both of whom failed so dis- mally at the War Office, are said to be opposing him strongly. Hence the postponement of Mr. ARNOLD FORSTER'S statement on the whole ques- tion in the House of Commons, and the very little light thrown upon it in the House of Lords on Tuesday. It is now rumoured that the postpone- ment will be extended to next year. In any case there is no chance of passing any scheme. this session with all the Government Bills so lamentably in arraar. A well—consid wed plan founded on the Esher report, would have been more welcome at any rate than the Licensing Bill. It might have diverted attention from some of the most flagrant blunders of the Cabinet, and have given one Minister at least the credit which his colleagues hunger for in vain. But it looks as if Mr. BRODRICK and Lord LANSDOWNE would not let Mr. ARNOLD FORSTER enjoy this happy distinc- tion. Besides, the Government have contrived to excite so much of the distrust which the " Stand- ard " calls" public prejudice," that it is probably too late for them to profit even by good intentions. They must hold together. The danger that Mr. ARNOLD FORSTER will resign rather than yield to the eminent failures who pre- ceded him at the War Office is not great. Mr. BALFOUR is likely to exercise his adroitness in composing the differences in the Cabinet. But the popular temper which comes out in Devon- port election marks the exhaustion of the public patience. The "Daily Telegraph" says it is "silly to pretend that adverse bye-elections furnish any excuee for the resignation of Ministers." But they do furnish a reason for believing that the country would rather get rid of these Ministers than wait for them to settle their private quarrel about Army Reform. After so many rebuffs from the constituencies they certainly gain neither a moral nor even a tactical advantage by remain ing in office.

All hope of any workable understanding bell come to between the Liberal and Labour partiee..lr Warrington seems to be at an end. What the opinion of the Education Committee leally is on the question of closing schools in times of epidemic sickness. Profiting by their ex- perience in the past, the Education Committea Is not satisfied that the suggested closure of the 8410018 will have the desired effect. This opiiiioo Will.be shared by many outside the Committee, for it is difficult to conceive that the children are IeSS liable to come in contact with other children 39,h00 out of school than in it. The Medical ufacer himself seems to hive some doubts on the taatter for on looking at his last published annual lep3rt we find that he refers to the wholesale Closing of schools on the occasion of an epidemic 91 measles in 1901 and concludes with the follow- _ing significant sentence: "On the whole i think TAW one cannot feel very certain about the value of the action taken."

The Medical Officer's suggestion has at any rate bad the effect of directing publi3 attention to the health of the town. The Education Committee has taken a step in the right direction in recom- mending the free use of the baths to ,the school children, and we sincorely hope the Hall, Parks and B sths Committee not place any obstacles in the way. -J-1i.13 popularisation of the baths amongst school Children will not only give every child an oppor- tunity of learning the useful art of swimming, but it will improve the health of the youngsters and teach them habits of tileanlinass which will More than compensate the town for the ,small monetary loss which the free use of the oaths will entail. It might not be out of place for the Education Committee to go further in the !same direction. They might put in a word for the nagged Snhool, which is doing noble sanitary Work in the way of washing slum children, and is compelled to pay for the water used. If the Baths are made free to school children, then indeed will 114 be the height of abstirdity—if it is not now—to Make Mr. Skelland pay for the water he uses in Promoting personal cleanliness and health among the poorest children of the town.

The result of the contest in the Harborough division is another heavy blow against the pro- tectionist campaign. Although Mr. Philip Stan- hope, the Free Trade champion, was an entire stranger to the constituency, and in that respect at a great disadvantage compared both with his ?PPonent, Mr. Harvey Dixon, and the late niem- oer, Mr. Logan, he has won the seat by the largest , taa3ority ever recorded in the division. With all !Ile great popularity, Mr. Logan only won in 1900 _?3' 1,330 votes, whereas Mr. Stanhope has piled up a,738 votes to the good. It is to bl hoped that the Protectionists will perceive in this signal de- feat the futility of such tactics as those adopted by Mr. Dixon. While avowing himself a follower of Mr. Balfour, and declaring that the very idea of a food tax "stuck in his throat," he allowed the 'constituency to bb flooded with the litbrature of the Tariff Reform Leagub, with whose objects he 7et professes to have no sympathy. Anything ,lese in harmony with Mr. Chamberlain's appeal to zde followers to" keep their banner high" it would be difficult to imagine.

There can no longer be any doubt that we are 111 for a renewal of the Protectionist agitation last autumn's campaign resulted in so complete an overthrow of Mr. Chamberlain that many came to the conclusion that he would accept defeat and devote his energies to some new project. But he Protectionist leader is an obstinate man, and ne is not yet convinced that the country has fin, ally rejected his mischievous schemes. Fortified aY large sums subscribed, to use his own favourite phrase, " gentlemen with foreign names," he la making preparations for giving the public a llooond edition of the "raging,tearing propaganda" Uring the next few months. Even the Tariff Reform League, hustled out of one constituency after another, is putting forth new signs of life. Jill this outpouring of energy and money means that. leree Traders must once more prepare to defend their position against the attack of the Protection- ists.

THE NEXT WARRINGTON OPERA. "The Gondoliers."

Our readers will be interested to learn that the big success of "The Yeomen of the Guard " g_iven last autumn at the instance of members of the Warrington ITisical Society, is to be followed next October by similar performances of "The Gondoliers." This vivacious opera—one of the most popular of the Gilbert and Sullivan collaboration.s—is, we understand, now well advanced in rehearsal, great enthusiasm marking the twice-5-week meetings which are yet. in the height •of summer, being held in the Cairo-street schoolrooms. The more numerous dramatis personae of "The Gondoliers" gives opportunity of utilising the abilities of a greater number of the Musical Society's mem- bers in the cast than was necessary last year —albeit it will be seen from the details given below that almost all who then so greatly distinguished themselves are included in leading farts this year. "The Gondoliers" will, we believe, be presented with even greater completeness than characterised the "Yeomen of the Guard" performances --the scenery, stage accessories, the dresses and the lighting, being of the very best that money can provide. The rehearsals of the music and the speaking parts are, !,8 last year, being studied under the direction of Mr. P. H. Crossley and presently Mr. Thornley-Dodge—to Whose abilities as dramatic instructor and stage manager ranch of last year's success is attributable—will take an intimate part, in preparing the opera for public perform- ance.

LOCAL NEWS.

Coming Events. The second anniversary of the settlement of the Rev. Herbert Dunnico will be held to-morrow. In the morning, service will he held in G-olborne-street, and in the evening Mr. Dunnico will preach for the last time until winter in the Parr Hall. The choir and children will sing at all services. Conductor, Mr. T. Brough, organist, Mr. A. Trow. The Rev. R. Wilkins Rees will preach on "The New Methodist Hymn Book" at Bewsey-road Wes- leyan Church, to-morrow (Sunday) evening.

Bold-street Wesleyan Sunday school will be re-opened after renovation to-morrow (Sunday) after- noon. A special children's service will be held, Hr. W. Roberts, J.P., presiding. The band of the Ardwick Green Industrial school will play selections from 3to 9 p.m. on Walking Day in Bewsey Hall Park. • - -

At Wycliffe Church to-morrow (Sunday) evening the special choral service "The Daughter of Jairus " will be rendered. Mr. A. Corney will deliver an address.

The Wages Dispute in the Flint Glass Trade. The glass-workers at Messrs. Robinson, Sons and Company Limited at Bank Quay, in common with the other glass workers in Lancashire, have not yet arrived at a settlement of their. dispute with the masters as to wages.

Another Case of Anthrax in Cheshire.

.case of anthrax has occurred on a farm at Whitley, Cheshire. The disease was detected in a, cow, the carcase of which has been crenalted. Is3la- tion precautions have been taken.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
The Volunteer Encampment.

The Warrington Volunteer Battalion will this year form part of the 30th field army brigade which is to be encamped at Salisbury Plain (eastern portion). The other battalions • forming the brigade will be the Second Cheshires, First Royal Welsh Fusiliers, and the Second Battalion Herefordshire Regiment. The advance party leaves Warrington on the 19th of July, and the battalion will follow on the 23rd. They will remain until the 6th of August.

The New Isolation Hospital at Dutton: Two foundation stones of the new Isolation Hospital to be erected at Dutton by the Runcorn Rural District Council, were laid on Thursday afternoon in the pre- sence of a large assembly,Mr. Thomas Wright presided and the stones were laid by Mr. S. Woodward and Mr C. E. Linaker, jun., who were presented with silver trowels and mallets. Afterwards tea was served in the Board Room at the Workhouse. Tho. present scheme will cost over £6,000.

The Social Service League. Another maeting of the committee was held last evening, the Rev. H. Dannico presiding. The con- stitution, rules &c. were definitely agreed upon and they will appear in our next issue. The object:of the league is the re-adjustment of the present social order" and the organisation is to consist of all per- sons, irrespective of creed or politics, who are in sympathy with its expressed objects. The annual subscription will be is. The Rev. H. Dannico was elected president, and Messrs. E. Osborne and J. Morton, secretaries. The following were elected on the committee :—Messrs. E. Lloyd Jones, E. England, J. Elwell, Nathan c3ook, N. Balmford, J. Heywood,and Councillor James Evans.lt was decided to ask the two lady Guardians (Mrs. Clay and Miss Broadbent) and a number of prominent .townspeople, irrespective of party politics, to become members of the committee.

Marriage at the Parish Church. The marriage was solemnized at the Parish Church on Thursday, of Miss Evelyn Dixon, daughter of Mr. John Dixon, Manchester-road, Warrington, and Mr. Arthur P. Marshall, son of Mr. James Marshall, late of Rock Ferry, Liverpool, and at present resident in Russia. The Rev. A. Pitt, vicar of St. Clement's, Liverpool, officiated. The bride was prettily attired in a white silk dress, trimmed with Brussels lace. She wore a tulle veil with wreath of orange blossom, and carried a lovely bouquet of white roses and lilies of the valley. Her ornaments were a gold necklet and pendent, the gifts of the bridegroom. There were two bridesmaids— Miss Amy Dixon and Miss. C. Dale whose dresses were of pink silk voile, trimmed with ecru lace:. They wore large black picture hats and carried bouquets of sweet peas. The bridegroom's gifts to the brides- maids were gold bangles. Mr. Stanley Marshall discharged the duties of best man. After the cere- mony the company adjourned to Cairo-street schools where the reception was held. Later in the day the newly-married couple left for Torquay where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's going away dress was a blue costume with hat to match. Police Promotion.

On the recommendation of the Chief Constable (lir Luke Talbot) the Watch Committee made several promotions at their meeting on Thursday. Detective Barlow, who has seen thirteen years service, was advanced to the rank of first class staff sergeant. He is a very useful member of the force, and already holdsthe positions of coroner's officer,assistant hackney carriage inspector, registrar under the Motor Act, and inspector under the Children's Employment' Act. Detective Forster, who has served nearly thirteen years, was promoted to the rank of second class staff sergeant. For some time he has acted alternately with Sergeant Williamson, as officer in charge of the bridewell on night duty—a very responsible position. It was decided that P.C. Clough be attached to the detective department. He has been connected with the force for fifteen years, and for a considerable period has acted as plain clothes officer and pro- bationtx detective. He was recently awarded the badge of merit for exceptional diligence. Sergeant Williamson was advanced to the rank of staff sergeant, with a plain clothes allowance. He is a smart officer, having been on the force since 1894. He obtained his promotion to sergeant after less than three years' service.

Presentations to Mr. G. F. F. White. Mr. George F. F. White, magistrates' clerk, whose marriage to Miss Bowden will take place on Wednes- day, July 6th, was the recipient of a present from the borough justices on Monday morning. The magis- trates met in their private room at the Police Court at half-past nine, and his Worship the Mayor presented Mr. White with a solid silver tea and coffee service, which is a beautiful specimen of the early Georgian period with richly chased edges and plain fluted body. Each piece bears the following inscription :—" Pre- sented by the Magistrates of the County Borough of Warrington to Mr. G. F. F. White, their clerk, on the occasion of his marriage, July 6th, 1904." The articles were supplied by Messrs. Eustance and Com- pany, goldsmiths, &c., Sankey-street. The Mayor spoke in high terms of Mr. White's services as clerk, and wished him "long life and happiness." Compli- mentary speeches were also made by Messrs. F. Monks, John Fairclough, J. Rymer Young, and Alder- man Hutchinson. Mr. White suitably replied. The Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. Stanley Wilson) was among those present.—Yesterday Mr, White received another present at the hands of the journalists of Warring- ton. The presentation was made in the Press room at the Police Court, Mr. J. Lawton, as the senior journalist, handing to Mr. White a handsome portable writing-table clock, suit- ably inscribed. He referred to the courtesy which Mr. White has invariably extended to the representa- tives of the Press, and expressed the hope that he would have a long and happy married life. Mr. H. Isherwood added a few words, and Mr. White briefly acknowledged the gift.—The clock was supplied by Messrs. Eustance and Company.

Personal.

_ Major R. N. Pochin retired from the regular army on Saturday, and took up a commission in the old 2nd Royal Lancashire Militia at Warrington. lie joined the East Surrey Regiment in February, 1885, and from 1899 to a few months ago was adjutant of the Volunteers at Kingston-on-Thames. The promotion of Lieutenant Tom M. Broadbent of the 3rd Cheshire Regiment to a captaincy is announced in the" Gazette." 'The regiment is at present in camp at Conway. The Rev. R. H.
