Jolley, one of the newly-appointed magis- trates, was sworn in at the Borough Court on Monday morning.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63. SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Sir Gilbert Greenall's Hunters.

At a sale of hunters at Tattersall's in London, on Monday, several hunters sent up by Sir Gilbert Greenall commanded high prices, Lucky Jim, a bay gelding which has carried Lady Greenall being run up to 400 guineas. Another Forge Accident. Thomas Bennett, (44), 9, Chapel-lane Stockton Heath, met with an accident whilst following his employment at Dallam Forge on Monday morning. He was conveyed to the Infirmary where one of his legs had to be amputated. Borough Accountant of Leigh. The General Purposes Committee of the Leigh Town Council recommends the appointment of Mr. A. Duncan, of Wolverhampton, to be borough accountant in succession to Mr. W. E. Houghton, who is going to Southwark. The salary is £2OO a year, rising by annual increments of £25 to £3OO.

An Age of Subordination. Dr. Goodrich, the chairman of the Congregational Union, was in a pessimistic mood on Thursday. Addressing a gathering of the Lancashire Independent College to celebrate the sixty-first sessionary anniver- sary of the institution, he likened the present century to an age of base subordination, declaring that "we have subordinated the child's education to the priest- hood, the China.rnan to the interests of the million- aire, and we are subordinating the people's morals to the brewer." He also expressed the opinion that secularism had to some extent invaded and depraved the churches, making them into "very ordinary insti- tutions with a financial committee."

UNDER the CLOCK for GOOD BOOTS.—Advt. A Compensation Claim Settled. Before his HonourJudge:Bowen Rowlands K.C.at the Warrington County Court on Thursday, Sarah Aishen, widow, residing at 51, Orford-lane, Warrington, I claimed under the Workmen's Compensation Act for the loss of her son, Frank Aishen, 15 years of agil, ' who was killed on March 15th whilst working on the premises of the Frodingliam Iron and Steel Company, at Frodingham, being then in the employ of the Pear- son and Knowles-Coal and Iron Co.—Mr. Cuthbert Smith, barrister (instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel) appeared : for the applicant and Mr. Ashmead Elliott, barrister, Manchester (instructed by Messrs. Payne, Galloway and Payne, Manchester), for the respondents.—Mr. Smith said the parties had agreed to save his Honour the trouble of hearing the case. Mr. Elliott had . acted with that generosity which always distinguished . him in matters of that kind. They had agreed to an . award for the applicant for £7O, and costs on the usual scale.—His Honour entered the award as agreed upon. Hairdressers and the Walking Day Holidays. At a meeting of ithe Warrington Hairdressers' Society held on Monday the 20th inst. the President Mr. A. Lamb presiding, the question of Hairdressers having a Aoliday on the Saturday after Walking Day, came up for discussion. It was pointed out that there was a general exodus from town on that day, very many taking advantage of the annual excursions from the works to have a day at the seaside. This caused the Hairdressers to have a tedious day,of keeping their saloons open with no work to do after the early part , of the day, as most of the men requiring shaving who remained in town attended early, not being engaged at their usual occupations on that day. It was also noted that several of the members of the Society took advantage of the quiet state of trade following after Walking Day and closed their shops for a consecutive , holiday of two or three days; and a holiday on the later part of Saturday would give them an oppor- tunity of getting away a little earlier. Ultimately it was resolved that the shops be closed on the Saturday- after Walking Day at 2 pm., and the hope was ex- pressed that customers would assist them to carry out the resolution by attending as early as possible on that day. Account Books Missing. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge B wen Rowlands, K.C., the case was again brought forward of Hull Bros, assignees of C. J. Dibb official• receiver, in the bank- ruptcy of P. W. Hallwood against William Keenan, of Hood-lane, Great Sankey.—Mr. Jones, solicitor, of Messrs. Leak and Pratt, Manchester, appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. A. Browne defended.— Mr. Jones now applied that the case he adjourned sine die. He pointed out that the matter was ad- journed at the previous court in order to enable him to find some account books which were missing. A search had been made but without result.—Mr. Browne said he must ask his Honour to dismiss the action with costs. The plaintiff had not an atom of evidence to produce in support of the claim. —Mr. Jones said his Honour would remember that defendant's daughter signed an agreement for the payment of a certain sum per week. If it had not been for that the plaintiffs would not have spent £6 or £7 on the case.—Mr. Browne observed that the case had been before his Honour on five distinct occa- sions, and each time it had been adjourned at the request of the plaintiffs.—His Honour said he would , non-suit the plaintiffs, with leave to bring a fresh I action. Addition to Queen's Gardens. There was a pleasing little ceremony on Thursday afternoon, when the Mayoress (Mrs. Bolton) formally opened the new extension to Queen's Gardens. The land, which has been very neatly laid out under the supervision of Mr. Parker, the head gardener, adjoins Springfield-street, and is entered from that thorough- fare. The gardens are now two acres in extent, and the latest addition will no doubt be appreciated by the townspeople. There was a good attend- ance of members of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee to witness the opening ceremony. Alderman Smethurst, the vice-chairman made a brief speech and handed to the Mayoress a silver key bear- ing the following inscription: "Presented to William Bolton/Esq., J.P., Mayor, by the members of the Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee on opening the addition to the Queen's Gardens, War- rington; June 23rd, 1904." The Mayoress having opened the park- gate, said she had very great pleasure in declaring the grounds open— The Mayor thanked tho members for their gift and alluding to the new grounds, said he thought the trees and shrubs would compare favourably with those in any other portion of the town. Considering the gardens were in the midst of buildings, he thought it 'was a very suitable place to have an open space.— The committee then inspected the grounds, and at the invitation of the Mayor afterwards adjourned to the Town Hall where light refreshments were served.

The Examiner, Saturday, June 25, 1904

The King's Birthday. In honour of the King's birthday the flag was hoisted at the Town Hallyesterday.

Bicycle Theft. At Manchester on Thursday, James Wallace Jones (20), Ashton-in-Makerfield, was fined £5 for stealing a bicycle at Urmston. Defendant, who hired the bicycle for a week, sold it to a Warrington broker.

New County Schools. The Lancashire Education A.utliority has decided to build two public elementary schools—each to accommodate 1,000 scholars—at Droylsden. The transfer of the Wesleyan Scho)l, Brierfield, near Burn- ley, has been accepted.

Sir John Brunner and the Licensing Bill. Sir John Brunner, M.P., replying to a Winsford resolution of protest against the Licensing Bill, said he agreed with it all except one clause, which stated that the measure was framed in the interests of brewers and publicans. This he strongly dissented from. The measure was certainly not in the interests of publicans._ _ _

See BOOTFI AND SON'S Latest Designs in Children's Boots and Shoes for Walking Day. Also the White Heather and Portland Boots and Shoes for Ladies' Wear. Battermarket street.—Advt. The Proposed Memorial to the South Lanca-

shires.

A meeting of the Workingmen's Committee in con- nection with the memorial to the South Lancashire Regiment was held in the Town Hall on Thursday evening. Mr. Davies preided.—lt was reported that the sum of £7l 2s. had been 'deposited in the bank to the credit of the committee.—Mr. Rope, (hon. treasurer) stated that he had since received a fifth instalment from Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co., and a first instalment from the Warrington Wire Rope Works Co.—The Chairman said the Mayor had addressed a circular letter to the gentlemen of the town and neighbourhood who had not subscribed to the fund. The committee did not intend to let the matter drop until every method of raising sub- scriptions to this very desirable object had been ex- hausted. Mr. C. J. Newton (hon. secretary) read a letter from the St. Helens Cable Works stating that the workmen declined to subscribe as trade was so bad and wages so low, that it was with difficulty they maintained their families. Reference was made to I the stirring poem written by Alderman Bennett, which appeared in the : Examiner "last week, appealing on I behalf of the memorial fund. It was decided to have I 1,000 reprints for circulation at the works.—Reports were represented by the delegates from Longford gas works and the tramway men as to the starting of sub- 'scription lists.—The Chairman said he believed an effort would be made amongst the clerks and shop assistants of the town to raise funds for the memorial It was decided to• ask the Warrington Football Club, the Police Athletic Club and the Cycling Club to lend practical assistance to the Memorial fund.

Claim for Dilapidations. His Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C. was en- gaged for several hours on Thursday afternoon at the Warrington County Court, in hearing a claim which bad been made by John Eden of Penketh, against James Pownall,formerly of. Fleathside Farm, Peaketh, but now of Lleweni' Flintshire. The claim amounted to £l3 13s. 10d, made up as follows damages to hedges and £l5 6s. 10d,dilapidations to house and buildings, less £3 135., credited to the defendant on contra accouut.—Mr. E. A. White, solicitor, repre- sented the plaintiff and Mr. Madden (instructed by Messrs. W. D. Jeans and Son) appeared for the defendant.—ln stating the case for the plaintiff, Mr. White pointed out that the defendant was formerly a tenant at Heathside farm under the plaintiff. From March 1893 to February 1902,the panties were on good terms, and the plaintiff on many occasions helped the defendant on the farm and also made him an allowance of cash for repairs. In February 1902, plaintiff gave notice to quit but with- drew it at the request of the defendant. It was arranged that half an acre of land should be given up for building purposes on the following February. When February arrived, the defendant gave notice of his intention to give up the tenancy, and the plaintiff promised to make an allowance in respect of the building lands if the defendant left the premises in proper order. Under the tenancy agreement the defendant was bound to keep the premises &c. in good condition and repair. When the defendant left, it was found necessary to spend considerable sums on repairs and it was also found that he had damaged the hedges by nightsoil. To recover the loss, the present proceedings were instituted.—Mr. Madden cross- examined the plaintiff at great length and proceeded to argue that on the authority of cases he quoted, the defendant was not bound to leave the premises in other than tenantable order, and that he had left them in such order and condition. Mr. White contende d that the chief case quoted by Mr. Madden did not apply, as in that case the covenant was to leave the property in good and tenantable condition, but in this case the covenant was to leave it in good condition. —His Honour upheld Mr. White's view on the ques- tion of law, but considered that the plaintiff had made out his claim on too lavish a scale. He allowed 30s. on the claim for damage w to hedges and £9 2s. for dilapidations with costs. On the counter claim he allowed the defendant £4l3s.—Mr. White applied for a special order for an extra allowance for the plain- tiff's solicitor, and this was allowed.

The death occurred with painful suddenness, on Sunday, of Mr. Charles Powell, 91, Bewsey-street, clerk in the office of Messrs. W. and J. Richardson, coal merchants, Marson-street. On Sunday night Mr. Powell returned from a short walk and was retiring to bed when he was taken suddenly ill. Although Dr. Adams was immediately called in, Mr. Powell died a few minutes afterwards. Baby In a Hand Bag. An inquest was held at Warrington on Saturday touching the recovery of the body of a male child from the Bridgewater Canal.—P.C. Hardy stated that he found a hand bag in the canal, and on opening it discovered that it contained the body of a baby. Dr. Smith said that from the appearance of the internal organs,•he was of the opinion that the child was still- born.—The jury returned a verdict to that effect. Musical Successes. Music Licences for the Holidays.

A number of local licensees made application at the Borough Police Court on Monday for music licences to be available during the fourteen days commencing on Monday next. The Chief Constable pointed out that at the annual brewster sessions the music licences of a large number of houses were refused, and the applicants were told that the Bench had power at any time they thought fit to grant a fourteen days' music licence for special occasions. He had no ob- jection to the present applications.—The magistrates granted licences for fourteen days to the following : Thomas;Walsh,Crown and Sceptre,Buttermarket-street; Thomas Cooper, Blue Bell, Horsemarket-street ; Robert Perdue, Higher Angel, Buttermarket-street ; Elizabeth Plinston,King's Arms, Buttermarket-street ; Ann Anderton,Cross Keys,Buttermarket-street ;George Lang, Rose and Crown, Bridge-street ; John C. Rigby Queen's hotel, Sankey-street.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING or TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warringtoll.—Advt. The Forge Fatality. - - -

An inquest was field at the Police Station on Saturday, concerning the death of Mark Dolan, aged 49, a coal heaver employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, and lodging at 120, Winwick-road. Mr. Henry Greenall was present on behalf of the tirm,and Mr. A. Lewis, H.M. Inspector of Factories, watched the proceedings.--The evidence of Peter Kelly, coal heaver, with whom the deceased lodged, showed that deceased was employed at Bewsey works. At ten o'clock on the previous Thursday morning Kelly saw him in the act of " barring " a waggon of slack, so that he could get it opposite a certain furnace. About 10.30 Kelly went round the waggon and found deceased had been crushed between the waggon and the end of the casing of the boiler. Dr. Adams was called in, but life was extinct. Witness was of the opinion that deceased went round the waggon, which was on a slight incline, to apply the brake, and was crushed.—The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."—The foreman (Mr. Whittle) remarked that some of the jury seemed to think there was not sufficient space for deceased to pass round the waggon, but he (Mr. Whittle) did not see how that was to be remedied.—The Inspector said it would mean the re-arrangement of the whole of that part of the forge. A suggestion which he thought the manager would agree with, was that where possible the levers of the brakes should be lengthened.

Funeral of an Army Veteran., The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Tuesday of Mr. John Wilson Alexander, a member of the Liverpool Corps of Commissionairs. Deceased who was sixty years of age, was formerly colour- sergeant in the Bth Battalion King's Liverpool Regi- ment,and for eleven years acted as sergeant-instructor to the 4th Volunteer Battalion King's Liverpool Regi- ment, at Bootle. He served with the colours abroad for a considerable period and was the possessor of the long service and good conduct medals. The funeral was attended by Mrs. Alexander (widow), Mr. Philip James Smith Morley (nephew), and Mr. Walter Brearley Batley (nephew),and Colour-Sergeant and Mrs. Waddington. There were representatives of the Liverpool Corps of Commissionaires, to which deceased belonged, consisting of Sergt.-Major Davies, and Sergeants Sanderson, McCaffrey, Higginson, and Greasley. The 4th V.B. King's (Liverpool Regiment) were represented by Sergeant-Instructor Needham, Colour-SergeantWaddington,Armour-Sergeant Wilkin- son, and Mr. Davenport,late sergeant. Amongst those present from Orford Barracks, Warrington, were Mr. A. Day, (late sergeant-major King's Liverpool Regi- ment), Bandmaster Wainwright (King's Liverpool Regiment), Mr. H. Chamberlain (late quarter-master Bth Regimental District), Sergeant Wilson (King's), Mr. Lake (late orderly-room-sergeant 3rd South Lancashire Regiment), and Mr. A.. Hardy (late quartermaster South Lancashire Regiment).

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of " Search Lights 03 Land, Labour • and Social Problems." AN EXPERIMENT IN TAXATION.

"There is a certain level which I think might be well observed in every civilised community as a cardinal principle of ifinance, that those who have to maintain their households should be as free as possible from any taxation whatever. That prin- ciple has been so followed out in the policy of this country as carried into law,that it is almost correct to say that there is no country in the world where those who have to struggle the hardest for sub- sistence are less opposed by the tax-gatherer than theyare here." Hon. G. H. Reid, Premier of New South Wales.

lam often asked for a few practical examples of what the taxation of land values has done to revolu- tionise any country, or to improve the lot of the masses of the t people. It is one thing to advocate this method of raising a nation's revenne,and to tell men what grand things it can ds in laying public burdens on the right men's shoulders, and in drawing our wealth from Nature's own storehouse, by a system of enlightened and beneficial taxation, but it is another thing to show us where these things have been tried, and what results they have produced, aud how the people regard such taxation when they have once given it a fair trial. The colony of New South Wales is a delightful example of what our blatant Imperialism, and our dis- honest systems of taxation can do to ruin a new country, and to blast its reputation for ever, and to prove our unfitness to rule the world.

1.-WE BEGAN TO BUILD THAT COLONY WITH BAD MATERIALS.

For many centuries our forefathers had a perfect mania for discovering and appropriating other countries in the ends of the earth, and neglecting their own fields and gardens at home. They sent Captain James Cook on a voyage round the world to pick up any stray con- tinents, or derelict islands he could find, and claim them as our own private property. In 1770. he explored the coasts and harbours of New South Wales, and he promptly hoisted the British flag as a sign of ownership. It is true there were thousands of natives who owned it, and occupied it long before Captain Cook discovered it, but they were only niggers to be shot down and exterminated whenever they became trouble- some. _

We had no particular need of this new country, and it was a white elephant on our hands, for we could not persuade Englishmen to go and live there. At last some genius conceived the idea of making it a penal settle- ment, and a dumping ground for our thieves and murderers that we did not send to the gallows. In the year 1788, we sent out from England his Majesty's ship " Sirius, " and nine other vessels to Botany Bay, with a cargo of 750 convicts. That place was found unsuit- able, so the commander of the expedition dropped anchor in the harbour of Sydney, and dumped down his "undesirables " there. These poor wretches had been sentenced to penal servitude for life, or for a long term of years, and we were manu- facturing criminals so fast in England that we were at our wits' end to find room for them, so we hit upon the brilliant idea of sending them to develop a new country In Australia. These thieves, and murderers, were the real founders of the colony. They felled the trees, and cleared the forests, and ploughed the fields, and made the roads, and built the houses, and turned the wilder- ness into a fruitful field while British troops kept them at work at the point of the bayonet, and shot them down if they tried to run away. If Imperialism means empire-building by violence, and robbery, and murder, lam not having any. The empire that is built by wars of aggression, and cemented with blood cannot last long. And I am dead against the building of any colony with such cheap and nasty , materials as convicts, and murderers, and thieves. If you corrupt the fountain of a nation's life you will utterly ruin the stream. If you build a State on rotten foundations and with "undesirable" materials, you must expect that fabric to totter to its fall. And yet we permit every new colony to be flooded with wastrels, and scoundrels, who are no credit to anybody, and we pay these criminals to fight our battles, and loot and plunder our enemies, and commit nameless atrocities for us. But there came a time in the history of New South Wales when men of character were wanted, and it was no easy matter to tempt them to settle there. Where is the honest, intelligent farmer who would take his wife and daughters to a colony of criminals, and reprobates and ask them to live among the scrum and froth of our volatile race? The colony had an evil reputation, and character counts for something among nations, as well as individuals. So the settlers we wanted had to be bribed, and tempted by all kinds of grants,and induce- ments or they would never come at all. The leavening of that mass of corruption cost us a pretty penny from first to last. We had to give them free grants of land for theirgarras and sheep ranches.We had to offer them free passages from Europe to the colony, and guarantee them an abundance of rcheap la- bour. The convicts were" apprenticed "to the farmer. I like that word. It sounds so much nicer than the word slaves. Each farmer took one or more of these criminals, and gave him his food and clothing and in return he took out of the wretch'all the labour he eould get. lI.—WE PERMITTED•THE CLASSES TO OPPRESS

===== 0002601_19040625_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
TEIE MASSES.

It needs no gift of prophecy to foretell what was sure to happen under these circumstances. The con- victs and their children were practically doomed to servitude for ever. They had to spend their days in grind- ing slavery, while their employers obtained free grants of land, and became rich by slavelabour. These employ- ers became town councillors, and members of Parlia- ment. and they made some nice laws for their own benefit. Th ‘y relieved the land of rates and taxes, as etrr Tory Government in England has done, and they levied duties, sent up the price qf food and clothing, and vastly increased the cost of living. These Colonial landowners became Fair Traders, and Protectionists to a man, because the system enriched them by impro verish- ing everybody else. One result of this misgovernment was the locking up of the land in a few hands, and the creation of a great artificial land hunger. In 1895, more than half of all the land in New South Wales was owed by six hundred and forty-two individuals,while nine hundred thousand people had not land enough in which to dig their graves. Another result of this misgovernment was the banish- ment of the settlers, who could not obtain the land they required. Let me rub in the moral of the official re- turns. We have been responsible for the government of New South Wales for 114 years. Its area is 310,700 square miles, and its population only 1,357,050 souls. It is six times as large as England, and there are not half as many people in it as there are in the West Riding of Yorkshire. It is one-third larger than either France or Germany,and it has little more than half the popula- tion of the city of Paris. Its revenue is £9,572,912 a year, and its debt last year was £63,762,066. So it costs every year £7 per head for every man, woman and child in the country to run the show, and if the debt were fairly divided among them,everybody would owe £47. I commend these figures to those howling idiots who are trying to cram their Imperialism down our throats, and who are everlastingly telling us that we are the born rulers of the world. We have been ruling New South Wales for one hundred and fourteen years, and if our statesmen can produce no better results than these they are not fit to be trusted with the management of a rag and bone shop. Things came to a crisis about ten years ago. The whole colony was on the verge of bankruptcy. The land was in the clutches of the money-lender, and the land-grabber, and it paid little or nothing to the revenue. Frantic efforts were made to induce emigrants to settle there, but it was like pouring water into a sieve,and the able-bodied workers left the country faster than they came, so the population was steadily falling, and the colony was drifting to ruin. lII.—TAXATION OF LAND VALUES WAS NOT TRIED TILL ALL OTHER METHODS HAD FAILED Even the landowners and the law-makers themselves were at their wits' end. Land was fast going out of cultivation, and being given over to the rats and the rabbits. The revenue was steadily declining, and the debt was accumulatingat analarming rate. The skilled workman who had been tempted to the colony by high wages, and then bled unmercifully by indirect taxation, took to themselves wings, and flew away. Something must be done to prevent the colony from bleeding to death, so in 1892, a resolution was adopted by the Parliament of New South %Vales in favour of a land tax, that would reach every land-owner, and make him pay for his land whether it was in use or not. This resolution led to a hot and angry controversy that lasted for two or three years, but the land-owners got the worst of it. In 1895 an Act was passed abolishing taxation on personal property and improvements, and taxing the value of all land for colonial purposes, at one penny in the pound. And it is significant of the popular feeling in the colony against the moneylender, that the same Act imposes a stiff income tax on mortgages, and imposes a heavy burden on those financial parasites who suck the life out of the land and give it nothing in return. Of course there was a great outcry among the land- owners in the colony. They said it was robbery and confiscation, and thieving, and it would bring the colony to ruin. The ploughshares would be rusty. The barns would be empty. They predicted that the land tax would make land dearer and that the landowner would simply increase the farmer's rent, and thus indirectly drive the settlers from the soil. We have heard all these yarns before, and we know there is not a word of truth in them. Every one of these prophecies has been falsified by the results. Let us see Iwhat effect this new land tax had on the big absentee land-owner, who held 100,000 acres of land, and spent his rents in London or Paris, or at Monte Carlo. If I his land we worth only £5O an acre he would have to pay five million pennies a year in

taxation, whether his land happened to be in use or not. If that man had to pay £20,000 a year for the fun of keeping land that he could not put to any profitable use, what do you think he would do with the land? He would sell it, or it would mighty soon eat its head off. And sell it he did. Many large estates of 100,000 or more came into the market, and were eagerly bought up in small farms for cultivation. In one instance there were 150 applicants for seven parcels of land. Within nine months from the passing of the Act 2,516 families were placed on the soil, and were living on their own little freeholds. Within two years the area of land under cultivation had been in- creased by no less than 311,500 acres. Now an Act of Parliament that can }vork such social miracles as these, in so short a time, must not be despised. _ _ _

If we had access to our own soil, and could turn out our own unemployed to scratch their living out of the soil when all other trades failed them, we should work a mighty revolution in this country, and we should settle most of the burning social questions that are troubling us to-day. If all the land that is now sacred to deer and partridges and hares and rabbits were used to feed and shelter men and women, we should hear less about slums and overcrowding and the housing problem. If every good ground maintained its man, we should have fewer com- plaints of hunger, and nakedness, and want of employ- ment. Let us see what the taxation of land values did for New South Wales. It sensibly reduced the cost of living, by abolishing many of the taxes on food and clothing, and laying these burdens on tie land. It broke up the large estates, and caused them to be offered in small lots to suit cultivators, because these were the ,men most eager to buy. There was a big demand for the good farming land that was thrown upon the market by non-using holders, who had held it up for specula- tion. On account of the rush for allotments it was necessary to ballot off among the applicants the various sections thrown open, and in every case the demand exceeded the supply. The new law has opened up virgin forests, and mines, and unoccupied land everywhere, and found plenty of work for lumber men, miners, quarrymen, builders, and farmers. The "Sydney Telegraph" makes fun of the predictions of ruin that were made before the law was passed, and it asks: "Where are the rusting plough- shares, and the empty barns that were to bear witness to the effect of making large owners of land share more equitably in the burden of taxation? None of these things have appeared as yet. The effect has been exactly the opposite of what the objectors prophesied." * *

And Mr. Reid, in speaking to the effect of the taxa- tion of land values in the colony, said: small as it is, it has done two great things—it has secured to this country for all time a good sound principle of taxation, and it has killed the trade of the land-grabber."

FESTIVITIES AT APPLETON HALL.

Lord and Lady Acton Welcomed.

The prettily situated and well wooded grounds ad- joining Appleton Hall were the scene of great rejoicings on Thursday and again yesterday when Lo rd and Lady Acton were welcomed on their first visit since their marriage to the ancestral home of the bride. By the tenantry on the estate Lady Acton is held in great esteem, and various useful organizations in the neighbourhood have found in her a warm friend. The re- ception accorded to Lord and Lady Acton on their arrival from London on Thursday afternoon was marked by the utmost cordiality,and the occasion will be remembered as a red letter day. The weather was ideal, the sun shining radiant' y,wh ilst a pleasant breeze tempered the heat. Over 500 invitations hadibeen issued;to tenants and employees, members of Mrs. Lyon's mothers' meeting, members of the Girls' Friendly Society of Appleton and Stockton Heath, members of the Appleton Habitation of the Primrose League, and members of the Stretton Mutual Improvement Society. At three o'clock the extensive grounds were thrown open, and it was indeed pleasant to while away the time in such surroundings. At the entrance to the drive, an arch,decked with ivy, had been erected, and the sentiments of the people found expression in nicely worked devices with such words as "Long life to the bride and bridegroom." Lines of bright streamers spanned the drive, and the hall itself was tastefully decorated, the word Welcome" appearing over the doorway. In the grounds there were various means of amusement. The Warrington Borough Band, under the conductorship of Mr. T. Griffin, discoursed sweet music, and many of the visitors tried their skill at the shooting saloons, and had a merry time in the swill,*b boats. At about a quarter to five ahearty cheer heralded the arrival of Lord and Lady Acton. They had driven from the station in an open carriage, and when the lodge gates were reached, the horses were dispensed with, ropes were in readiness, and a contingent of young men—mem- bers of the Mutual Improvement Society—pulled the, vehicle along the drive to the hall. The band struck up "See the conquering hero comes" and there was more cheering, Lord and Lady Acton bowing their acknowledgments. Lord and Lady Acton and the whole of the guests then adjourned to a marquee where tea was served by the Warrington Cafe Company. The house party entertained by Mr. and Mrs. T. H. Lyon, consisted of Colonel the Hon. B. Herbert, Hon. Miss Annie Dalberg Acton, Miss Langford Brooke, and Mr. Harold Thorpe. Others present were :—The Rev. the Hon. C. F. Cross, Mr. and Mrs. Usher, Mr. Colin Mac Iver, the Rev. F. and Mrs. Evans, Mr. and Mrs. Whitley. Lady Acton was prettily dressed in embroided cloth, with white hat trimmed with roses and boa. After tea, short speeches of welcome were de- livered by the Rev. the Hon C. P. Cross and Mr. T. Hall. A number of presentations were then made to Lady Acton, who gracefully acknowledged the gifts. Mr. Joseph Pennington asked.the bride's acceptance of a gold bracelet set with diamonds from the Cheshire and Lancashire tenants. Mr. Matthy, the butler at the halls presented a turquoise and diamond ring on behalf of the indoor and old servants. Mr. James Parkinson pre- sented a pearl and sapphire pendant from the outdoor employees on the estate. Mr. Ditchfield presented a jewelled necklet on behalf of the Stretton and Appleton Habitation of the Primrose League. Miss Brockle- hurst handed Lady Acton a silver shoe horn, button hook, curling lamp and tongs, the gifts of the mothers' meetings. Mrs. Whitley presented a copy of George Eliot's works bound in white vellum from the Girls' Friendly Society (Stockton Heath, Appleton Thorn, Stretton and Whitley) ; and Mr. William Holland, on behalf of the Young Men's Mutual Improvement Society (Stretton) presented a swan fountain pen. In thanking the members of the Primrose League Habitation for their present, Lady Acton expressed her regret at having to give up any active work in con- nection with the habitation. She wished she had been able to do more. Lord Acton, she might say, belonged to the other party. (Laughter.) She had had some very interesting political discussions with him, but so far neither had been able to convert the other. (Laughter.) She was as staunch a member of the league as ever, although she would not be able to do any more work.

In thanking the Mutual Improvement Society for the gift of a fountain pen, Lady Acton said the members could not have given her a more appropriate present. The wife of a diplomat had to write long letters, and she believed she would be allowed to help in copying dispatches if her handwriting was good enough. So the members of the Society would be helping the King's service through the pen.

Hearty chee'rs were given for Lord and Lady Acton, and in a brief speech of thanks, Lerd Acton alluded to the great happiness it had affordel him to come to Appleton and see the esteem in which Lady Acton was held by her old friends. Tt might be said of her that she was "a prophetess in her own country." (Applause.) During the evening the band played for dancing on the lawn, and a thoroughly enjoyable time was spent. Yesterday afternoon the school children of Stretton and Appleton were given a handsome treat in the grounds.

SUN'S RAYS TO MELT METALS.

Senor Himalaya, a i"o-it-uine-se--piise-s-t—ir-o-i-r; Lisbon and a professor of science in a college in Oporto and Coimbra, has,' states an American contemporary, arrived at• the St. Louis fair with a novel invention by means of which he promises to melt anything by the agency of the sun's rays. Space has been granted him where he will instal his mammoth machine, weighing 30 tons, and occupying 6,000 cubic feet of space. The maghine is a system of giant lenses, which concentrate the rays of the sun from a large area at a focal point.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A HEARTLESS THEFT. What the Chief Constable described as a heart- less theft was investigated by the Warrington magistrates on Monday when Frederick Meadow- croft was charged with stealing a 10s. postal order, the property of Sarah Speakman, his married sister, of Banner's Court, Brick street.—The facts of the case, as stated by the Chief Constable, were that prose- outrix had been deserted by her husband. and on Sat- urday her sister, who resides in Runcorn, sent her a letter containing a 10s. postal order. Mrs. Speakman was not at home when the letter arrived, and prisoner opened it, abstracted the order and cashed itat Church-street post office. When prosecutrix taxed him with the offence, he gave her Bs. It was a very heart- less theft, for the prisoner practically lived upon the woman and anyone else who would keep him. He was I:kinking most of his time.—Prisoner was sentenced to three months' hard labour.

TWO MONTHS FOR A PROWLER—A PARTING THREAT. Richard Jones, who said he came from Bolton, was charged on remand at the Warrington Police Court en Saturday with loitering in Paul-street and Eustace- streot on Saturday, the 11th inst. with intent to commit a felony.—Superintendent Monahan stated that at 9.25 p.m. Ida Davies, aged 16, who lived at 6, Paul-street, saw the prisoner on the opposite side of the street, going from house to house, ringing bells, knocking at the doors and looking through the keyholes. Eventually he came to her house and rang the bell, and being frightened the girl went upstairs. She then heard a noise at the back door, and on going there saw the prisoner who said "Why didn't you open the floor? I rang the bell twice." The girl was too afraid to speak to the prisoner, and when her mother came in she told her of what had occurred. P.C. Sweeney arrested the prisoner in an entry near the house.— Prisoner said "it was a got-up case from beginning to end."—Superintendent Monaghan said the prisoner had been convicted,3s times. He was nothing but a travel- ling and prowling thief.—Prisoner was sentenced to two months' hard labour.—As he was being removed to the cells, he shouted to Superintendent Monaghan, "When I come out you'll be dead ! " The superintendent smilingly remarked "I'm not frightened."

GIRLS' ADVENTURES.—TWO small girls named Sarah and Margaret Collins, both of 13, Arpley-walk, were summoned for wandering abroad not under proper guardianship.—lnspector Hackett of the N.S.P.C.C. said that one day in March the girls tramped to Manchester. On their return they were picked up by the police at Hollins Green, and handed over to the Borough police, when they made an allegation that their father had ill-treated them, a statement which they afterwards admitted was incorrect. All through the following night they sat on the doorstep, and the next day went to Liverpool and tramped about Liverpool until they were eventually taken in at the workhouse. The Inspector saw them in the workhouse, and they told him they had taken their father's clothes to pawn, and with the money had gone to Manchester and Liverpool. Up to a fortnight ago they had behaved rather better, but they were now as bad as ever, and had again taken their father's clothes to pawn. They were incorrigible.—Mr, William Bamber, school attendance inspector, asked or. the children to be sent to St. Joseph's Industrial School Darlington, and the magistrates agreed.

THE CHIEF CONSTABLE'S EXPERIENCE.—AIbert Walters, a stranger, was charged with having been drunk and begging in Sankey-street on Tuesday after- noon.—The Chief Constable said he was in Sankey-st. at two o'clock and noticed the prisoner, in a state of intoxication, annoying foot passengers. He stopped them in an impudent way, and several people had to leave the footpath to avoid him. On see- ing him (Mr. Talbot), prisoner shouted to him, then stood in front of him, and it was a case of "Stand and deliver." Prisoner said "I want something to eat. What are you going to give me ?" Witness told him that he was a police officer, and that prisoner must not conduct himself in that way in the streets. Prisoner walked on a little distance, and then returned and began to be very abusive. Re (Mr. Talbot) again warned him that he would lock him up, and prisoner then snatched his (Mr. Talbot's) walking stick from under his arm and threw it on the ground. Witness collared the prisoner who was very rough, but fortunately two officers came upon tke scene and he was taken into custody. Prisoner was an able-bodied man and had money in his possession. He was one of those class of people who would not work, and yet their stomachs were always full of meat—and very often of drink—while honest working men with families could hardly make ends meet.—Fined 10s. and costs or 14 days' imprison- ment. _ _ _

SOMETHING DIFFERENT IN AFTER- DINNER SWEETS.

Something different in after-dinner sweets may be prepared day after day by consulting Brown & Poison's recipe book "Summer Dishes." The recipes which have been prepared by skilled cooks, are for suchhealth- ful dishes as "Creamy Custard," Blanc-Mange Sponge," "Natural Fruit Jellies," "Fruit Juice Sauces," "Prune Jelly," and the celebrated "Brown & Poison Blanc- Mange," with full instructions in serving it with all kinds of stewed fruit. The basis of the recipes is sweet milk, fruit, and Brown & Poison's" Patent "Corn Flour. The booklet is sent by return to all applicants sending Id. stamp to Brown & Poison, Paisley. Write for it 1101 V —yon may forget it to-morrow.

HOW HOUSEHOLDERS ARE DUPED. A new method of swindlinz has been adopted, which is being carried on to a considerable extent throughout the country. It appears to be the work of a gang, the members of which have carefully planned and organised the system. To advertise their Specialities a large firm of Soap Makers have inaugurated Prize Competi- tions, and are giving valuable prizes to their customers in exchange for wrappers or coupons. The next Com- patition closes on;August 3:st. The mods operandi of the tricksters is simple; two smartly dressed young ladies or men, carrying bags, call upon householders, introduce themselves as representatives of Messrs. Joseph Watson & Sons, Ltd., and state that they have called for any wrappers which have been saved for the Prize Competition just about to close. The swindlers intimate that the wrappers will be placed to the credit of the householders in the forthcoming Com- petition, and the result made known in due course, or they will offer a few trumpery articles in exchange which are of no value. It is to be hoped, however, that this warning will set householders on their guard. All wrappers must be sent direct to Whitehall Soap Works, Leeds.

Funeral of the Late Mr. John Mather.

- On Sunday afternoon the remains were interred in the Warrington Cemetery of the late Mr. John Mather, 198, Knutsford-road, who for the long period of 59 years had been in the service of Messrs. Stubs and Company, file manufacturers,Scotland-road. The deceased was one of the managers of the War- rington Permanent Benefit Building Society, and most regularly attended the meetings of the managers. The Examiner, Saturday, June 25 1904

EDUCATION COMMIT:2E.

THE SUGGESTED CLOSING OF SCHOOLS.

Discussion on the High Death Rate. MR. A. COW'S NEW APPOINTMENT.

A meeting of the Warrington Education Committee was held on Monday evening at the Town Hall. Mr. F. Monks, J.P., occupied the chair and there were also pre- sent :—Hr. Alderman Bennett (vice chairman), Revs. Father Wilson, John Yonge, and E. J. Willcocks, Alder- man Pierpoint, Councillors Dodds, Burton, S. Davies, Brough, Ellison. Lord, Roberts, Tinnion, Hulse. Messrs. A. J. Jolley J. Upson, R. T. Fakelough, G. Walker, E. Plinston, T. May, C. E. Unsworth, W. Bibby, J. Fulton Smith, with .T. Moore Murray (director), and J. Fair- hurst, (borough treasurer). THE TRAINING OF TEACHERS.

The Director read a letter from Mr. H. E. Clare, clerk of the Lancashire County Council, stating that at the adjourned conference between the representatives of the County Councils and the County Borough Coun- cils of Lancashire and Cheshire, with reference to the provision of training colleges for teachers, held in .Man- chester on June 9th, the following resolutions were passed :—(1) That the number of trained teachers em- ployed by the various elementary education authorities should be largely increased; (2) that it is desirable the various educational authorities in the whole of Lancashire and Cheshire should co-operate in the train- ing of teachers and in the provision of training colleges, and that a special committee be appointed to draw up a scheme for coniideration, carrying out the foregoing proposal ; (3) that the special corn nittee consist of two representatives from each of the County Boroughs,three representatives of the Lane ishire Education Committee, three of the Cheshire Education Committee, three representatives from each of the Universities of Man- chester and Liverpool, and a representative of each of the residential training colleges in Lancashire and Cheshire. Mr. Clare asked for the names and addresses of the two ineml)ers appointed to act on the com- mittee.

Mr. John Upson an Mr. A. .T. Jolley were appointed representatives of the Warrington authority on the committee.

FINANCIAL MATTERS.

MR. MAY WAN CS A COMPARATIVE STATEMENT. AR. GOW'S NEW APPOINTMENT. THE METHOD OF PAYING TEACHERS.

At a meeting of the Finance Committee, held on the 13th June, the members having considered a report by the District Auditor on his recent audit of the accounts of the •Education Committee, a discussion followed as to the method of paying the teachers' salaries. The committee recognized that considerable responsibili'y was placed upon the head teachers with reference to their returns of theattendance of the teachers at school, and, it being reported that inaccuracies and oversights had taken place in sending in these returns, it was resolved :—" That the Director be instructed to notify the head teachers that any oversights or inaccuracies in the returns of the attendance of all the teachers engaged in the schools will be reported to the com- mittee,and,unleas they are satisfied that there is reason- able ground for excuse, each case will be referred by them to the Education Committee for )consideration." It was resolved :—" That a new gate be provided at the Education Office in Sankey-street at a cost of 24 195."

Mr. A. J. JOLLEY moved the confirmation of the minute:l.

Councillor DODOS seconded, and remarked that the committee were in full sympathy with Mr. May's idea of getting comparative figures. At present, however, such figures would not be of much use. Mr. WALKER said he did not se any reason why the committee should not have a complete return of the teachers employed in the town. It would be a difficult matter for a member to state, if asked, the number of teachers amongst whom the salaries were divided. If they had a comovative statement of the number of teachers employed when the committee "took control of educa- tional matters, alai the number on the list to-day, together with the salaries paid, they would be able to realize to some extent how the money was being expended. It might be an easy matter to make thestate- meat show not only how much of the increase was due to in creased staff, but how much was due to advance of salaries. Mr. T. MAY said he would move a resolution to that effect. The apparent increase is salaries to teachers as compared with the corresponding month last year, was £1,272. He said " apparent" because some of the mem- bers had told them that there was no means of "com- pare." He did not intend to ask any special considera- tion from the committee, but it would be an assistance to the members and the ratepayers, who were the main consideration, if they knew how the money was spent from time to time instead of waiting till the end of the year. There had been no budget statement for the last completed year ended 31st March last, and they had all been lo oking forward to it. The CHAIRMAN: It was in the papers. It is no use making a statement like that, when it is not correct. Mr. MAY : It was merely the auditor's statement There was no detail whatever.

Councillor LORD considered the price of the new gate at the Sankey street offices was exorbitant. In fact, he did not see the necessity for a gate at all. The CHAIRMAN called the attention of the committee to the fact that they were beginning to feel the demand for officials in their employ. Mr. Herbert Parkinson had obtained agood appointment in the South of Eng- land, and it would havl been seen from the papers that they were losing Mr. Gow's valuable services. He was afraid that that would go on, as the salaries they were paying would not keep good men. They would be con- stantly changing, he was afraid, if they did not mind. The Rev. J. YONGE said he had been thinking about the matter since they received the intimation of Mr. Gow's appointment at Blackburn, at twice his present salary. He viewed the fact of his removal with a very great amount of regret—(hear, hear)—and that feeling would be shared by anyone who knew anything about the facts.. They had found in Mr. Gow an exceedingly efficient master. He had had to manage matters at their initiation, and, if he might be allowed the phrase, to lick them into shape, and that work he had done most efficiently. He (Mr. Yonge) believed he was say- ing what others would corrobarate emphatically, that there was no particular point in which Mr. Gow had come short of' their expectation. (Hear. hear.) He had very likely gone beyond what some of them expected he would be able to do. He (Er. Yonge) had been wondering whether any plan could be hit upon by which they could retain his services. It might seem rather a large order, but he submitted that it was worth con- sideration. He did not suppose they could offer him £500; the committee and the Town Council would not be likely to swallow that particular idea, but he wanted to know whether something approaching that could not be done. To have a new man in the second year of the secondary school would be a very risky experiment. Methods would have to go under alteration, and altogether it would cause a feeling of unsettlement and unrest in the whole working of their educational scheme. He thought if the School Management Com- mittee could receive a"hint that they would be justified in taking the matter into consideration • it would NI a very good thing. If they parted with Mr. Gow, it stood to reason that they would not be able to appoint a successor at £250. They would have to pay a new man more to begin with. Mr. Gow, he thought, was now entitled to a larger salary, and in the coming year there would be' abundant opportunity and justification for giving him even more than £3OO. The CHAIRMAN observed that the matter was not on the minutes, but it was of such importance that they might venture to ventilate it.

Mr. JOLLEY said he would prefer to say nothing about the matter which had been raised by Mr. Yonge. It would be more fittingly raised when the minutes of the School Management Committee were considered, although he entirely ageed with every word of com- mendation of Mr. Gow s admirable services. Anyone who had taken a personal interest in the welfare of the secondary school, technical institute,and pupil teachers' centre must know the very thorough way in which Mr Gow had taken his work in hand and the admirable spirit in which he had carried it out. With regard to. the suggestion as to comparisons, that was How really a stale subject,but when suggestions were put forth which were calculated to mislead the public, he supposed it was his unfortunate duty to correct mis-statements. No useful comparison between the figures of expendi- ture for the past month or the current month and for the corresponding period of last year could be given by anyone. He said that without the slightest fear, and for Mr. May to get up and say that the salaries for the last month were £1,200 more than for the corresponding month last year, without any further comment, was highly misleading an' utterly unfair. Mr. Walker had made a suggestion with which he (Mr. Jolley) was in sympathy, and he could assure him that as soon as it was possible and practicable for the committee to give information which would be useful and accurate—and information which was not accurate could not be use- ful—they would give it. (Hear, hear.) He thought Mr. Walker would concur that,as chairman of the committee he (Mr. Jolley) tried when possible to give all the information in his tower. Any member could see the schedule monthafter month at the office. The question of the number of teachers on the staff was within the province of the School Management Committee. The members of the Finance Committee had enough to do to look after their own business without interfering with the work of another sub-committee. There had been increases of the staff, but the fact had been men- tioned in the minutes month after month. With refer- ence to Councillor Lord's remarks, if they did not hive a gate to keep the children from the vacant land they would have to employ a man for the purpose. The minutes were carried. Mr. MAY rose to speak. The CrTA MN( : Sit down, Mr. May. You have al- ready spoken. Mr. MAY: I protest against the word " mis-state- meat," as applied to my remarks. I said "apparent increase." - -

Alderman PIERPOINT : You should have objected at the time, not now. The CEIAIRMAN.—You have already spoken, Mr. May. You make mis-statements by the dozen.

INIPORTANr BUSINESS. THE SUGGESTED CLOSING OF SCE-100LS. HOW IT ‘VOULD AFFECP THE HEALTH OF THE TOWN. THE ACCONDIODATION AT THE SECONDARY SCHOOL.

The School Management Committee had had impor- tant business under consideration.—"A letter dated the 28th May, 1904, from the Medical Officer of Health, suggesting the desirability of closing all the public elementary schools in the borough at once until the date already fixed for their re-opening after the midsummer holidays (15th August), owing to the continued preva- lence of infectious disease, was mil) flitted, and it was resolved that having taken into consideration the fact that 21 departments have already been closed daring the present school year for periods ranging from two to font weeks at a time, and that, during the ten weeks ending the 26th March last, 1,125 (or eight per cent). of the children have been excluded from attendance at school by the direction of the Medical Officir of Health as suffering from, or being in contact with, infectious disease, this committee is not satisfied that the suggested closure of the schools will have the desired effect." A further letter from the managers of Fairfield National School, asking (a) for reconsideration of their request for permission to appoint an additional certifi- cated assistant mistress in the upper girls' department ; and (b) for permission to appoint an additional certifi- cated assistant mistress in the girls' department, was submitted. With reference to the application in respect of the upper girls' department, it was resolved that a sub-committee, con :i3tin; of the Chairman of the committee (the Rev. Morley Stevenson), Mr. A. J. Jolley, Mr. J. loulton Smith and Mr. John Upson, be appointed to confer with the managers of the school, with a view to considering the advisability of re- organizing the upper girls' and girls' departments. With reference to the application in respect of the girls' deptrtment, the committee were of opinion that the staff of that department required strengthening, aml, in order that advantage might be taken of the fact that teachers would be soon leaving college and would be available after the midsummer holidays, it was resolved that permission be given for the appointment of an additional certifictted assistant mistress; and that the decision as to the department to which this teacher should be finally attached be deferred until the receipt, of the report of the sub-committee appointed to con- fer with the managers of the school.

It having been reported that applications had already b43en received for the admission of 100 scholars (whose parents are either ra.tep tyers or residents in the borough) to the Secondary school and to the preparatory pupil teachers' classes, it was resolved that in order to acorn- modate 60 additional scholars (a) rooms Nos. 8 and 9 be divided by means of glass screens; and (b) art assistant master, qualified to teach chemistry, physics, and general subjects and an assistant mistress who can teach drawing french and general subjects be appointed to the Secondary school at salaries of £l2O and £llO per annum respectively. It was resolved that application be rade to the Town Hall, Parks and Biths Committee for the free use of the second-class public swimming baths by the children from the public elementary schools in the borough at times to be fixed, the children to be in charge of a teacher.

In moving the confirmation of the minutes Mr. UPSON expressed the hope that the Chairman of the commit- tee, the Rev. Morley Stevenson; would speedily be restored to health and streneth and be amongst them again. (Hear, hear.) With reference to the resolution relating to the Fairfield school he had to explain that the committee had not yet met owine to the illness of the Chairman, and the absence of several members of the committee. In regard to the suggested closing of schools owing to the outbreak of infectious diseases, the School Management Committee had been charged with being interested only in grants and averages. He did not know that they were altogether had things to be interested in seeing that they had the interests of education and the rateyavers in their minds when deal- ing with these grants and averages. While that was so, they were still more intimately concerned with the interests of education, as such, and the closure of the schools for a prolonged period very adversely affected the interests of education. It did not affect the grants. If all the schools were closed that day for the next three months by the sanitary authorities of the borough not a penny piece would be lost in grant, nor would tbe average go down. On the other hand, it was possible the grants would be higher and the average greater if the schools were closed, so that grants and averages were not their primary considerations. They thought primarily of the education of the children. The schools had been closed during the year for a considerable time and the committee felt that the education of the chil- dren would suffer terribly if they were closed again. However, these considerations were as nothing when they thought of the health of the people of the borough. (Hear, hear.) If they were •convinc3d that the health of the children would suffer in consequence of keeping the schools open, so far as the committee were con- cerned they would no doubt meet the sanitary authori- ties of the borough, but the code required that the schools should be closed for epidemic sickness not by the Education Committee, but by the Health Com- mittee. They were the responsible persons, and if two members of the Health Committee acting under the advice of the Medical Officer, said that the schools must be closed that must be done at Once. The Education Committee had no option in the matter at all. The CHAIRMAN: We can appeal. Mr. UPSON : Yes, but we are bound at the time to close. Continuing, he said, the question arose "Who is responsible for the closing of the schools ?" They, as the Education Committee, said that it was not part of their duty to close the schools because of epidemic sickness. The Health Committee was to some extent shirking its responsibility when it threw the onus of closing the schools upon that committee as the educa- tional authority. If any one must close the schools let the Health Committee do so under the advice of the Medical Orficer, The Education Committee would have the right to appeal to the Board of Education, but they had not got that far yet. The letter from the doctor did not say definitely that the Health Committee had come to any decision on the Matter at all. It was gathered that the matter had been discussed, but that no resolution had been passed, and no direct instructions had been given .to the doctor. Under those circum- stances he contended that the School Management Committee were justified in not agreeing to the suggestion that it was desirable to close the schools. Alluding to the proposed free use of the second class swimming bath by children, Mr. Upson said that at the I present time by the charge of a penny the very poor children, who perhaps needed the baths the most, were excluded. If the resolution was accepted and finally passed by the Council, they would give to every boy labove a certain age an opportunity of getting a weekly. I wash at any rate. Referring to the accommodation at ' the Secondary school, Mr. Upson said the success of the school was no doubt due largely to the earnest and efficient work of Mr. Gow. At the same time it had been crowded since its opening, and now there were 86 children coming in. They proposed to make provision for 60, and to do that they would have to make a few slight structural alterations, and engage two extra teachers. Then he thought they would be able to go on for twelve months without any further enlargement The buildings were being fully utilized. They had 120 children in attendance, and they proposed to admit 60 more. They had 90 pupil teachers, and 200 children being taught cookery, and in the night classes there were 631 students. In the course of a week 1,100 students of various kinds passed through the hands of the teachers. Councillor Burton seconded the confirmation of the minutes, and said the Health Committee had not come to any decision with regard to the closing of the schools but it was suggested by one or two of the members that they thought it would be advisable for them to be ' closed now until the end of the holidays. The death rate ef the town was very high, and, of course, the Health Committee felt rather nervous: They were anxious to do all they could to make the death rate lower and therefore they wrote to the Education Com- mittee. He (Councillor Burton) was in favour of what Mr. Upson had said shoe, the baths. He had been told by some of the teachers that some of the children were sent to school unwashed, without breakfast, which some took with them, and that instead of the teachers being engaged in teaching they had to take the _children into a back room and give them a good washing before the ordinary teaching was proceeded with. Alderman BEgNETr observed that the committee were incurring a grave responsibility in even seeming to go counter to the advice of the Medical 0 ficer of Health. The condition of the public health of the borough for some months past had been, well he might call it a scandal. He thought for thre3 or four times they had had the highest death rate in the country, and he saw from the ne.vsp Leers which were published on Saturday that the death rate for Warrington was 2i.G, and for Aston Manor 7. With such a divergence of that kind there must b 3 something 'radically wrong. He did not say who was right or wreng but the medical officer of health was responsible officially. The health of the borough primarily rested with him, and they _were in- curring a grave responsibility in even seeming to go counter to his advice. .The question of 4rants and averages was important, but the other question of edu- cation was more serious still. These long intervals in the school year must hamper the progress of education and retard the development of the scholars, but as Hr. Upson had pointed out, there was something infinitely more important than grants and averages or education and that was health itself. (Hear, hear) They could ~not educate people when they were dead. (Laughter). It was too grave a matter to laugh at. It was the absolute truth. (Re- newed laughter), Every week this year deaths had occurred; homes were being darkened, and families bereaved through deaths which under the present state of things should be preventable. They could not dogma- tize. It was extremely foolish when men who obvi- ously were not experts attempted to do so. They paid a handsome salary to a man Who was siOpoited to be an expert, and who was, no doubt, a capable public servant, and when he said it was desirable a certain course should be adopted in the interests of the health of the borough they should not throw cold water on it, and endeavour to prevent the adoption of the proposals. (Hear, hear.) He knew it was said that the remedy was in the hands of the Health Committee, but they were only human, and would hesitate to take a step which the Education Committee did not consider would have the desired effect. Whilst this hesitation was taking place, lives were being lost. Alluding to the question

of the proposed free use of the baths, the alderman said that was a matter that was connected to some extent with the last question. The heavy death-rate was partly due to the filthy conditions in which many of the people were compelled to live, and any step, however slight, which would tem. to greater personal cleanliness, would have a very considerable effect in the prevention of disease.He was not sure that the small second class baths would be adequate to the large demands which it was proposed to put upon it, but cleanliness and swimming were so desirable and important that they ought to make the necessary provision so that all the children could have the facilities both for cleanliness and exercise.

-- Mr. MAY said that reference had already been made to the death rate at Aston Manor which was the lowest in the kingdom. It so happened that his nephew was Medical Officer of Health there, and his father, the speaker's eldest brother, had been medical officer there for many years before him, and he (Mr.-May), knew the struggle this gentleman had had to bring the health matters of Aston Manor into a satisfactory condition. When he was with the Medical Officer there a, few weeks ago he talked over the matter very seriously with him and asked for his advice and enlightenment, and it was pointed out to him that the death rate from measles, whooping cough, and other infectious diseases mainly arose in children from three to five years of age who were crowded into these schools. He (Mr. May) came to the conclusion from the information the Medical Officer give him that no committee ought to continue to keep the schools open when the Medical Officer had made such a suggestion as they had on the minutes. (Hear, hear.) Without enlarging any further about the pros. and cons. which would go home to the hearts of all the members of the committee who had children and young children in their households, he proposed that the minutes be pissed except that particular resolution; that was, if Mr. Bennett had not already proposed it. Mr. MURRAY stated that from January 23rd to March 26th 1,125 children were excluded from the schools under certificates of the Medical Officer either for hav- ing been infected or for having come into contact with persons affected, and the loss of attendance during that time was 53,316. That was a loss of money to the com- mittee in the sense that those scholars did not earn grants. In October and November last, the schools were closed, some for a month, some six weeks and others for two months.

iqI:.:VIH.TON SMITH referred to the question of baths for school children, and said it would be very necessary to impress on the children that it was part of their education. When children were taken out of school through the streets they were likely to feel as if they were out of control. Mr, UrsoN, replying to the discussion, said the com- mittee had no desire whatever to thwart the doctor. With reference to the free baths it would be early enough to talk about the times at which the children should go when they got permission for them to go. In Liverpool the children went in school hours, and in other towns they went after the afternoon session. The boys went now between the hours of 4 and 6 on Tuesdays and Fridays. As Mr. Fulton Smith said the children must be impressed with the idea that the bathiug was part of their education. ----.- - - MAGISTRATES CRITICISED. TEIE SCHOOL ACCONDIODATION DIFFICULTY. _ _ . . . _ _ .

At a meeting of the School Attendance Committee on June 2nd, a letter (dated the 20th May) was read from the Board of Education, intimating that the average attendance at the Hamilton-street National school dur- ing the current year must not be allowed to exceed the recognized accommodation. Another letter (dated the sth May) was submitted from the managers of the Hmilton-street National school to the Board, disclaim- ing responsibility for the overcrowded state of the school, having been submitted. It was eventually re- solved : *"l7hat the managers of the Hamilton-street school be directed to see that the average attendance in each department during the current school year is kept within the limit of the recognized accommodation, and that the head teachers of the school be requested to furnish the committee from time to time with a list of the children o,ho have been refused admission to the school ;" and also "That, in view of the repeated noti- fications from the Board of Education as to the necessity for increased school accommodation, the attention of the Sites and Buildings Committee be drawn to the imperative necessity for making further pro- vision for school accommodation as speedily as pos- sible." _ _

CounciIIOrELLISON, in moving the adoption of the minutes gave a lovg statement of the facts of the recent prosecution of Mr. W. A. Proud for neglecting to send his child to school. The details have already appeared in the " Examiner." Councillor Ellison pointedi out that if a child had been prevented from attendance at school by sickness, that might be regarded as a reasonable excuse, but the onus of proof rested with the parent and after the experience of many years the only evidence that the School Attend- ance Committee could admit was that of a certificate, signed by a medical practitioner. A parent not observ- ing the bylaws was upon conviction, liable to a penalty. In order to avoid bringing all cases under that bylaw before the court, the committee met weekly and made preliminary investigation into the cases, and to avoid the appearance of parents in court, they accepted what they considered reasonable excuses. The only alterna- tive in the event of a person refusing to appear before them or to satisfy them as to the non-attendance of a child was to take the matter before the magistrates. That was done in the case under notice, and when the case was heard a medical certificate was produced in court giving proof that the excuse given for the child's absence from school was a reasonable one and as a con- sequence the case was withdrawn. The committee were now satisfied that the child was unable to attend school, but they felt that considerable trouble had been caused to both committee and parents which could have been saved by the production of the medical certificate on one of the numerous occasions upo t which it was asked for. The committee regretted that. two gentlemen whom they all highly esteemed and respected, holding the dignified position of magistrates and being members of that Education Committee, should have considered it a part of their duty to try and refute the efforts of this committee on the Bench by the use of words which would have been better not spoken. (Hear, hear.) Ti say the least they took it as a reflection upon the School Attendance committee and its staff in trying to carry out their arduous duties to the best of their ability. Another thing he wished to refer to was the question of school accommodation, as the resolutions passed on the subject referred toa very serious state of affairs. The committee would remember that at the meeting in March last it was reported that the Board of Education were withholding the annual grant for the Hamilton-street school, until they were satisfied that steps were being Laken to avoid the over-crowding in the school. In reply to that, the managers showed that the over-crowding was very slight, and they stated that any responsiblity in the matter should fall upon the authorities whose duty it was to provide school accommodation. This was communicated to the Board of Education both by the committee and by the managers of the school, and the Board, in reply, now gave definite instructions that the average attendance must not exceed the accommodation during the present year. The only way this could be avoided was to dismiss some children from the school, but if they turned to the monthly return of school attendance, they would find that at the Fairfield school (girls') there were 620 girls for 571 places, at the Ladies' School of Industry, 229 girls for 221 places ; at Silver-street school, 839 boys and girls for 777 places. at Warrington Parochial,7s4 boys for 670 places; whilst at St. Mary's R.C. Girls' school, there were 329 girls for 352 places, and at the St. Benedict's R.C. school 268 boys and girls for 320 places, but this last figure did not

the include Standard 1., which had had to be placed in the infants' department on account of want of room for is whole of it in the mixed department. The only selle/ in the district in which there would appear to he 's room were the two Roman Catholic Schools (St.garti.. and St. Benedict's),,and there could only be one so tion to the difficulty which confronted the Sch,7o Attendance Committee and the managers; V" of the committee could prove to the Board .ds Education that they were doing s mething to Pr°vl,..t additional accommodation, there was little doubt tu-t after so many warnings, the grant for Hamilton-streess school would be withilld, and that would mean a ,I°ia of over £1,200 to the committee.
