WARRINGTON FOOTBALL ASSOCIATION Last Season's Work.—The Annual Meeting

Mr. J. Harvey presided at the ann oil meeting of the Warrington and District Football Association which was held at the Patten Arms Hotel on Thursday evening.

THE SECRETARY'S REPORT.

The SECRETARY (Mr. T. P. Bradshaw) presented the annual report as follows : The Association last season was divided into fou divisions and the number of Clubs in each division was as follows: Warrington 19, Cheshire seven, St. Helens seven, and Widnes three, making total of 36 clubs, against 28 the previous season, an ncrease in member- ship of eight clubs. Out of that nmber 18 entered for the " Observer " Challenge Cup as against 16 the previous season. This Competition though not so successful financially as last year on account of the wet weather, proved to be a great source of attraction to the followers of the Association game, the matches being well fought, particularly in the Semi Final and Final Ties. The attendance of the members of the executive has been excellent and they deserve special praise for the way in which they have discharged their duties during the past season. I have again to refer to the great number of misconduct cases dealt with during the past season, which easily beats last season's record, 74 cases having bc3n dealt with. Two cases were dismissed, one, sending off the field considered sufficient punish- ment, four players were cautioned, 15 received seven days' susransion, 30 fourteen days, five one month, two six weeks, two end of season, 10 pending appearance, one player is under suspension until 30th of September next, a second player until 17th Septembbr, and a third until 14th September. One case was referred to the Army Football Council, end two cases were referred to us by other associations. In addition to above one linesman was cautioned. one club was suspended for fourteen days, two clubs were cautioned as to conduct of spectators, one club was ordered to have ground posted with notices, one club was cautioned for unsatisfactory way the minute book had been kept, one club was cautioned for unsatis- factory state of the cash book and failing to keep a minute book, and one club was fined for playing a man during his term of suspension. In the " Observer" Cup Competition one club was ruled out of the competition one match was ordered to be replayed for breach of rules, nine clubs were fined for late start, two clubs were fin c 1 for failing to send in result of match asre- quired by rule 12. This, I think, you will agree is a formidable list and naturally incurred a great deal of time and expense. You will observe from the figures stated above that severe sentences have in most cases been imposed,without the desired effect, but I trust that next season will in this respect show a vast improve- ment. Otherwise to cope with the difficulty, I am afraid that the closing of one or two grounds in the district must follow, as in the majority of cases the spectators are responsible for the conduct of the players, encomaging them time after time to defy the rulings of the referee, end it remains for the Committee of the Clubs in the district to help ' us in this resTect toi keep aheir spectators in hand, failing which, the measures before mentioned, will have to be put into effect. In my opinion the work of the Association could be made much lighter if the club secretaries would make themselves thoroughly acquainted with the laws of the game as revised up to date, and to acquaint their players of the various alterations and to dispense with the services of any player guilty of re- peated misconduct. The refereeing in the district is far from satisfactory e-ni this is a matter that will require careful consideration in the near future. At the com- mencement of the season the Referees Committee arranged for a series of lectures for the benefit of the referees, but advantage was not taken of the oppor- tunity afforded of gaining experience from well known and efficient referees and the lectures had to be discon- tinued. Financially, as you will see from the balance sheet, notwithstanding the deficit in the Cup Tie rem iptl as compared with the previous season, we are in a sound position, having a balence in hand of £6 s. 7d., and should we be more fortunate in our Cup Ties next season, we anticipate a handsome balance. The work of the Association is increasing year by year and the management expenses must increase accord- ingly. Mr. J. Fulton Smith, a gentleman well known and esteemed in the town, is nominated for the position of President and I have the assurance of Mr. Smith that if elected, he will do all in his power for the success of the game and I feel confident that the meeting will unanimously elect him to that position. The officers are all proposed for re-election, but I regret to inform you that Mr. Harvey cannot see his way to again accept office, through circumstances over which he has no con- trol, and asks to be relieved of that responsible position. Mr. Harvey has been Chairman of the Association for the past three seasons and during that period has shewn great tact and ability in handling the business of the Association.

On the motion of Mr. W. H. SMALLEY, seconded by Mr. T. P. FORBES, the report and accounts were adopted.

ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

The CHAIRMAN said that at the last meeting of the Executive Committee it was decided to ask Mr. J. Fulton Smith to become president of the association. Mr. Smith had written intimating that if the general meeting decided to appoint him, he would endeavour to support the association to the best of his ability. The chairman proposed that Mr. Smith be elected president for the coming year,and pointed out that he had already showed his interest in the game locally by presenting the cup after one of the final ties. They ought to feel proud that Mr. Smith had consented to occupy the position.

On the motion of Mr. SMALLEY seconded by Mr. BIBBY, Quarter-Master Sergeant J. P. Hollins, of the King's Regiment, was elected chairman. In returning thanks Quarter-Master Sergeant HOLLINS said that the clubs represented on the association covered a large area,and as he was only elected a member of the executive last year, he appreciated the honour very much.He believed he was the first soldier chairman of the association—and that fact would be appreciated not only by himself but by • the military. His heart and soul was in association football,and he would do all he could for its advancement in the district. Mr. T. P. BRADSHAW was re-elected secretary, and Mr. R. Hinds treasurer. The following were appointed members of the Executive Committee :—Warrington division, Messrs. W. H. Smalley, Arthur Titmoss, G. T. Bibby, J. Banks, and F. Foulkes: Cheshire divisiot, Mr. R. P. Booth. The appointment of representatives for St. Helens, Widnes, and Earlestown was left in the hands of the Executive Committee. Messrs. Bibby and Smalley were appointed auditors. The CHAIRMAN alluded to the strong hold which Rugby football had in Warrington, but pointed out that much enthusiasm was now being manifested in the. Association game. He hoped they would eventually be able to form a good Warrington team. A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Harvey for his three years' services as chairman, and he was elected a vice-president. The secretary and treasurer, and press representatives were also thanked. • The SECRETARY remarked that the association was working harmoniously with all the associations in the district. He thought the time was not far distant when they wotild have to appoint a new secretary, but during the coining season—which he hoped would be very uccessful—he would endeavour to discharge his duties aithfully.

Fatal Trap Accident.

Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, held anlinquest at the Police Station on Monday evening touching th(3' death of Mrs. Evelyn Constance Stanl4y Smith, aged 33, wife of Mr. Stanley Smith, wine and spirit merchant, Heath House, Croft.—Richard Stanley, coachman at Heath House, stated that at 10.30 on j Friday morning last week he left Croft driving a' single horse attached to a dog cart in which were seated the deceased and another lady. When passing ' under the Central Station bridge the horse slipped,and the occupants of the vehicle were thrown to the ground. Deceased was so seriously.hurt that she was at once removed to the Infirmary. Witness had driven the horse in question about twelve months and had been in the service of Mr. Smith for three years. He thought in the first instance the horse was startled by a passing train,^and then seemed to have lost its head. —lnspector Taylor said he witnessed the accident. The horse appeared to plunge and fall down. A train was passing over the bridge at the time. Deceased was thrown out of the vehicle and fell in front of the horse. Witness rendered first aid, and Mrs. Smith was removed to the Infirmary.—Nui se Dobson said deceased was admitted to the institution suffering from a fractured skull. She never regained consciousness and died early on Monday rnorning.—The Coroner said Detective Barlow informed him that Mr. Smith was so overwhelmed with grief at the loss of his wife, that he (Mr. Brighouse) came io the conclusion that no useful purpose could be served by bringing him to the inquest and adding to his sufferings.--A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned, and the Coroner I and jury expressed deep sympathy with Mr. Smith in I his very sad bereavement.

PIGEON STEALING AT WIDNES,

At Widnes, on Monday,Thomas Cassidy,2l, Catherine- street, was charged with stealing two pigeons from a stall in the Widnes Market Hall, the •property of Charlotte Jane Hoose. Superintendent Strickland said that Mrs. Hoose kept a poultry stall in the Market Hall. While her son was in charge of the stall prisoner opened a cage, took out two pigeons, and handed them to a boy, who ran away with them. Information was given to the police, and Police-constable, Davidson brought prisoner to the Police Station. When charged he said, "I did not steal any pigeons." Evidence was given by Harold Hoose and Police-constable Davidson, and a fine of 10s. and costs or fourteen days was inflicted.

THE BALFOUR CUP.

Won "ay Dr. Peacocke, of Warrington.

THREE PLAYERS TIE.

Our readers will be pleased to hear that the honour of winning the Balfour Golf Challenge Cup at Timperley, on Saturday, fell to Dr. W. J. Peacocke, a popular mem- ber of the newly-formed Warrington Club and of the Mersey Golf Club. ! The doctor tied with two others—C. Anderton, of Timperley, and W. Mowat, of Didsbury—and had to play off an extra nine holes. Curiously enough all three men were on the same mark—receiving six strokes, the handicap limit being seven. Dr. Peacocke's win was a popular one, for it was only by a piece of outrageously bad luck that he missed romping home with flying • colours in the first place. With the exception of a seven at the rather long second hole he played splendidly out and in until he reached the penultimate hole. He had then a gross score of only 74 against " bogey " 72 for the sixteen. The seventeenth hole is a fairly short one, and not by any means difficult, but Dr. Peacocke got badly bunkered, and before he had holed out eight strokes were down on his card, the hole being a " bogey "four. The eighteenth hole he did in three, making his gross score 85,- and net 79. His card read 5 7 4 3 4 3 5 5 5-41 4 5 6 6 5 4 3 8 3-44 85-6-79 ' Mr. Anderton played almost equally good golf, after an exceedingly bad start. For the first two holes he had recorded the unpromising total of 15. He appeared to be rather nervous at the start, but he pulled himself together afterwards, and returned a card reading :

8 7 $ 4' 5 4 4 5 6-45 3 4 6 5 5 5 5 4 3-40

85-6-79

These scores headed the list for a long time, though there were several better gross returns, until Mr. Mowat joined the pair with :- 4 4 5 5 5 4 4 5 5-41 435756 4 5 5 Ii

85-6-79

When all the other players were in, and it was seen that 79 had not been beaten' the trio started out to play a three ball deciding match over the first nine holes. Mowat began very badly. His first drive went out of bounds and he had to take another. On the way to the green there is a hedge, a ditch, and a belt of trees. Here again he got into difficulties, so that he had to drop a ball, and took seven to hole out. Anderton made a splendid start, and took the first three holes in 12, one better than "bogey," and two better than Dr. Peacocke, but the latter caught him at the fourth—where Anderton played badly on the green —and gained a stroke each on the fifth and sixth. On the seventh Anderton went to pieces, drove out of bounds, and afterwards got bunkered. With a lead of six at the ninth, Dr. Peacocke could afford to play badly, and he did. He missed a couple of putts, and took seven to hole out, three more than are allowed to "the Colonel." And still he won by 5 strokes.

The tie scores Were:— • Peacocke 5 5 4 4 4 4 4 5 7-42 Anderton 4 5 3 6 5 5 7 6 6-47 Mowat 7536 5 5 4 8 6-49

Perhaps one of the most significant features of the day's results was the thorough way in which the scores justified the handicapping. Following upon the three 79's were six 80's, seven 81's three 82's, thirteen 83's ten 84's, thirteen 85's, and so on. Six out of the seven previous winners were playing, but with the exception of Mr. Clegg, they did not dis- tinguish themselves. Last year's winner, F. Marsh, of Didsbury, did very well, with a gross score of 82. A. Ramm, Manchester, and H. Naylor, Timperley, were each 85, A. G. Hogg. Manchester, scored 86, and S. W. Gillett, Bowdon, did not return his card.

1 OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

I Sharp-street Primitive Methodist Mission. A sale of work was opened in Sharp-street Primitive Methodist Mission on • Tuesday afternoon with a view to augmenting the renovation fund. The opening ceremony was performed by Mrs. Armitage and Miss Bessie Dutton, and in the absence of Councillor James Evans, tke Rev. J. G. Reed presided. There was a fairly large attendance. During the aftennoon Miss Bridge sang "Dear Heart," being accompanied on the piano by Mr. B. Podmore. The stalls, five in number, were pre- sided over by Mesdames Wildman, Carrick, Meachim, Brown, Grady, Elwell, and Shaw.

New Roman Catholic Church. Sunday witnessed an event of considerable interest , to Roman Catholics in Warrington' the Right Rev. 'the • Abbot of Ampleforth (Father J. 0. Smith, Ph. D., • 0.5.8.) opening the new church of St. Benedict which has been erected in Orford-lane at a cost of £9OO. The ' church is a corrugated iron structure, situated behind the schools in Orford-lane and with a means of approach from Earle-street and Watkin-street. It will accom- modate about 500 worshippers. The interior is well • arranged, though nothing has been done in the way of decoration as yet. The choir are accommodated on a raised dais at the end of the church. All the seating and woodwork is of pitch pine. Prior to mama on Sunday, a procession, headed by the crossbearer and clergy, followed by the choir, started from the schools and walked in procession round the ehurch, the hymn "Come, Holy Ghost," and "The Asperges," being sung, the Abbot sprinkling holy water on the building. Having completed the circuit the procession entered the church by the main door and went up to the high altar where the Litany of the saints was • sung. While psalms were being recited the procession proceeded round the interior, the Abbot again sprink- ling the holy water. A fair congregation had assembled and the service proper began. The mass was sung by , the Abbot, who was attended by the Revs. Fathers N. V. Wilson, 0.5.8. (assistant priest), T. 0. Swarbreek, 0.5.8. (deacon), R. B. Primavesi, 0.5.8. (sub-deacon), and J. B. Gibbons, 0.5.8. (master of ceremonies). The Rev. J. W. Baines who will have charge of the church was also present. The sermon was preached by the Bey.R. ii. Annacker, S. J., of St. Francis Xaviers, Liverpool, who took as his text, ' For the expectation ol the creature waiteth for the revelation of the sons of God." Special music was rendered by the joint chair from St. Alban 's and St. Mary's churches,Mr. Eckersley conducting. Mr. J. B. Caldwell presided at the organ. In the evening the Sacred Heart service was sung, Father Swarbreck officiating. The Right Rev. the Abbot preached. The choir again rendered special hymns, and the" Te Down" was sung during Benedic- tion, which was given by the Abbot.

The Next Alderman.

Sir,—l should liketo know whether it is a Int that at a picnic of Tory workers on Saturday at Sonthport,the Aldermanic vacancy was discussed—and filled. it is rumoured that the Conservative choice fell upon Coun- cillor Forshavv ! What about the claims of men like Councillor John Evans (also a Conservative) and Coun- cillor Tinnion ? With due respect to Councillor Forshaw I am unable to see why, he should receive the Conservative nomination for the vacancy. Perhaps the rumour is no more than a rumour. In any case the choice of the Conservative workers does not necessarily settle the question.—Yours etc. Warrington. JUSTICE.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
The Death Rate —Closing of the Schools.

Sir,--At the meeting of the Education Committee held on Monday evening, a long discussion took place as to the cause of the amount of sickness amongst children, and whether it would be advisable to close all the day schools. I will not at present go into the pros and cons of what is the cause. but asso much rests on the shoulders and advice of one man (our Medical Officer of Health) I should like to know from the other medical men of our town what their opinion is as to the cause of so much sickness.. So important a step as closing the schools and compelling thousands of children to run the streets should not rest on the shoulders of one man. My strong opinion is that the remedy is not with the closing of schools, but from other causes not far to seek, and can easily be traced, but in all due deference medical opinion first —Yours Stc., ONE •OF THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
An Apology Required.

Sir,—Your correspondent, Mr. Joseph Podmore, junior, president of the Trades and Labour Council has again been unfortunate in his "seeming suddenness "as shown by his letter in the "Examiner" of Saturday last. Re commences his effusion by stating that a remark of mine is not true. I notice in reading the report of the Trades and Labour Council in the same Issue of your paper that he called someone else "a liar." That offensive expression is evidentlya figure of speech -with him.

Personally, I do not deviate from the position I have taken up, and emphasize the veracity of my statement, viz., that the reply I received to my question," How is it that you cannot accept a Progressive like Mr. Cros- Seld ?" as "that Mr. Arthur Crosfieldwas not a Trade 47nionist." Mr. Podmore also went on to say that Mr. Crodfield was not a member of any of the societies affiliated with the Labour Representation Committee. If he does tvot know already who stated that "there is not a good Capitalist "—he ought to know,considering he was there when it was made.—Perhaps he will be good enough to ask the "irresponsible member" who recently occupied the chair at one of the T.L.P. meet- ings held at Bridge Foot, and who cried it out in the Parr Hail a few Sundays ago. It is certainly unfortun- ate that such a silly remark should be attributed to the President of the Trades and Labour Council. However, Mr. Podmore knows full well who made it, and it is a piece ofjugglery on his part to endeavour to inveigle me into stating that I " implited " it to him.—Yours truly,

EDMUND OSBORNE.

29, Lovely-lane. Warrington, Jane 22, 1904.

The Poor Children's Camp.

Sir,—Dn Wednesday last I read a. report of the work of the Education Committee of this County Borough. That report shewed the position with regard to the heal th of the poorchildren. On the details I refrain from commenting. Years ago the Town Council adopted the principle of "Open Air Baths." 'Where are they? Recrimination and mutual distrust have flourished laxuriantly. Is there not room for human sympathy and "goodwill towards men." Show by your practical assistance that the influence of the example of the "Man of Sorrows "is still a living force. At night I was reading a work which it had long been my desire to read, "The life of Jesus" by that great Frenchman, Erneat, lifinan. Reading the chapter entitled "The place of Jesus in the history of the world," I could not help thinking, Where is the place of Jesus in 'the hearts ef Plea? The times are not, flourishing I admit. Should these considerations stay our hands in well doing ! Having regard to the Sanitary condition of the County Borough! To every other condition which should commend itself to the consideration of those who have the welfare of the poor children at heart, I trust that the scheme fortheir benefit will not suffer through lack of appreciation. Remember! From these children must come the toilers of the future, the lighters, the very backbone of the nation's vitality and greatness. Then please lend a hand in a work which must commend itself to your consideration. The same staff who cave their services last year have volunteered again—two 'tidies and four gentlemen Every care will be taken that money is not wasted.—Yours Szc., W. H. J. JENRINS, Hon. Sec.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
The Social Question.

Sir,—Your correspondent "—One Interested " has written two interesting letters on the" Social Brother- hood Idea." With much that he says Tam in entire agree- ment and if only he will enlarge the circle of his reason or at any rate refrain from narrowing that of others he will I am certain prove not onlya welcome but a worthy addition to our ranks. Like yourself Sir, he fights shy of our declaration of principle, "the re-adjustment Of the present -social order " and likewise is unable to supply a berttei. I venture to suggest that future cor- respondents save their time and your space by leaving that part of our programme severely alone unless they are in a position to substitute something better. I do not wish your correspondents to imagine that I resent criticism. I do not, in fact I welcome it. When an idea assumes great importance for a man his devotion to that idea is apt to hinder him from perceiving all that his idea has worked in the minds of others and on that account criticism is of immense value. At the same time his critics must bear in mind that possibly they have failed to track the thought which they believe themselves to be criticising. However in your next issue, if not in this, I hope to be able to place before your readers a copy of our constitution and I hope that until that appears "One Interested" will excuse a very busy and an extremely tired man if he withholds what some may deem necessary information. Permit me however to make a personal explanation. Your correspondent wishes to know why I publicly- identified myself with the Liberal party. Before reply- ing to that question mayl ask another? Is it not an Indisputable fact that the man who stands aloof from all political parties, however conscientiously, must face the risk of political impotence? I believe that it is; hence I have identified myself with that party because no other political organization is broad enough to in- clude me in its ranks. If this statement is challenged I am prepared to give chapter and verse. Meanwhile I com- mend the movement just initiated to the consideration of all who are engaged or desire to be engaged in varied forms of service for thewell being of the people.—Yours sincerely

Sir,—To 14 Free Critic " I give my best thaaks for the, 'friendly and courteous reply to my few remarks on his first article on the above subject. With reference to my friend's reply concerning the question of God and the Social Problem he will remember that I distinctly, pointed out thst the Brotherhood or Social Service League will have nothing to do with religion or theology as such, and seeing that it is in connection with Otis Social Service League we both write on this subject and taking into consideration also that both Christians and Non-Christians are to meet under the, banner of the League it worild, I 'think, be as well, and lam sure my friend will agree, that all reference to God be left out of his articles in fairness to those who, like myseli, take an interest in these things and are anxious to do some little to make things better, but who, nevertheless, do not agree in matters of religion , with those with whom they work or are willing to work with. I am glad also of" Free Critic's "assurance that it is not his own party he is concerned about, but about the welfare of the people of Warrington. If this is so he and I are on the same platform, and the question between us is now only a question of method arid policy best calculated to attain the object we have in hand. And our object I presume is the better representation of the labouring classes,and through this,und in all other ways possible, to improve the position, conditions and surroundings, etc., of the common people as a whole. Now, if we are to improve our representation at the next election it is quite clear that the Liberal and Labour parties should come to some agreement in order to avoid a three-cornered fight—and disaster. Whether this agreement is possible lam not so sure. To tell the truth I think it is not possible if the rumours con- cerning the intentions of the Labour Party are at all well founded. And we must not forget that there will be more than this next election, and if Mr. Crosfield gets defeated this time (which is likely) what will be the position at the following election to the one forth- coming ? The position will be this, that the Labour Party will have lost the opportunity of testing their strength and improving their standing and will also as a consequence be much weaker when they come to con- test their first battle at that time. If Mr. Crosfield stands alone and represents the progressive vote of the town has he any chance of success at the approaching election? If he has and will remain true to the labour interests-then by all means let him stand. But if the probabilities are that he will not succeed then I see no harm in the Labour Party having a test of their strength, and indeed if this is so I think the Liberals ought to stand down and support the Labour candidate. The chief difficulty in the way of the Labour party will be the securing of a suitable candidate and it is a difficulty not easy of solution. Remember I do not close my eyes to the fact that if both Liberal and Labour candi- dates are in the field defeat to both is well nigh inevit- able, but I have no hesitation in saying (it may -seem strange no doubt) that many who vote Conservative when Liberal and Conservative are opposed will vote Labour when a Labour candidate appears. However we shall see what both parties are inclined to do,but whatever decision they do come to I 'hope ltlwill be in the best interests of the people and not of the parties merely. Again thanking" Free Critic" for the friendly tone of his article and hoping also that he will take this as from one who with him desires not self interest,or party compromise, but the betterment of the affairs and con- ditions of the people of this our town and through them in time to extend operations over a broader field and a wider area.—Yours ONE INTERESTED.

NO COMPROMISE! Labour Representation Committee

Decline to Meet Liberals.

We have received the following communication from the Secretary of the Warrington Labour Representation Committee :

WA-R-R-I-IC-O-T-0-S LABOUR REPR.ESENTATION COMMITTEE. Dear Sir,—l have been instructed to forward to you a copy of a r.solution that was unanimously passed at a. meeting of the above committee held on Tuesday even- ing last, at which meeting, a letter from the Liberal Executive, asking us to receive a deputation, was read. RESOLUTIoN.

Inasmuch tliat the Rules and Constitution of this Committee strictly forbid any compromise or alliance with either of the two political parties, and recognising that our object is to form the Labour forces in Warrington into one united party; and also considering that our mem- bers hitherto have either been allied to or voted for the Conservative or Liberal parties, we consider it would not be permissible or wise for us to take any steps which would alienate any of our members from our Committee, which Committee is represen- tative of the necessity and rapidly growing Inten- tion of Labour, to be directly represented in Parlia- ment by a distinct and independent party, whose central policy shall be the Rights of Labour.— Yours truly, • 0. BUCKLEY. 3nne 23, 1904. IFor comments on the crisis in the Labour Party, and the revolt of Messrs. Crooks, Shackleton, and Henderson, see our leader columns.—ED. "Ea. 1

STOCKTON HEATH.

Continued from page 3,

THE ELECTRIC LIGHT QUESTION.—A meeting of the Parishioners will be held in the Schoolroom on Monday to discuss the application of the Warring ton Corpora- tion for permission to supply electric light to the inhabitants of the district.

SUNDAY SCHOOL TREAT.—The scholars of the Bap- • tist Sunday School, Hill Cliffe, had •',their annual treat if on Monday, when the pastor and teachers and friends I took them to Foxley Hall Farm, Lymm. Altogether , about 200 made the journey. The drive to Lymni was , pleasant, and when the party reached the field, buns and I milk were distributed and sports and games set in mo- I.tion, while some of the party rambled through the vil- lage. At five o'clock tea was provided in the Baptist , Schoolroom' Higher lane, under the directions of Mrs. ! Hughes, Mrs. Jones, Miss Moss, Miss Dodd and others, after which a return was made to the geld where the •various classes were given prizes for racing. Before starting on the return journey the whole company assembled in the yard of Foxley Hall Farm and sang " Lead, kindly light, after • which, on the motion of Mr. E. Lloyd Jones, seconded by Mr. Joseph Moss. jun., and supported by Mr. Shenton a vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Gould was submitted by the Rev. J. S. Hughes and carried with acclamation, three cheers being given as the party moved away. The journey home was much enjoyed and Stockton Heath was reached about 9.30. all having been delighted with their outing. Thanks and cheers were accorded to the following who had sent lurries and horses :—Messrs. JosenhlMoss, jun., William Moss, Septimns Moss, Messrs. W. and J. Strange, P. Cartwright and C. Hughes.

WHY WARRINGTONIANS DIE.

CAUSES OF THE HIGH DEATH RATE. -- Drink, Dirt and Slum Dwellings.

The high death rate of the Borough presents an opportunity for drawing attention to some of the causes which have led up to it. The suggestion of the Medical Officer of Health that all the public ele- mentary schools in the Borough should be closed with the:view of preventing the spread of infectious diseases amongst children can only be a precautionary measure, brought forward with the object of meeting the emergency which has arisen. The causes leading up to our present unsatisfactory position are deep-rooted. Measles and whooping cough are diseases peculiar to children of tender years, but we do not find the death rate in other towns jump- ing up to the topmost ring with such persistency as it has done in Warrington this year. How then are we to account for it? From what I have seen and been able to gather from those whose duty it is to go amongst the people—especially poor people—and see them in their homes, there is good reason to believe that drink and dirt and.sium dwellings are largely responsible for the prevalence of so much disease in the town.

• The home life is deteriorating under these evil influences and it is a source of wonder how some children are able to thrive at all. The increase of drunkenness amongst women is the saddest feature of the situation, and becomes the despair of all social reformers. The maudlin besotted.ness of some parents leads to a neglected home and the child- ren are the first to suffer. The National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children—the necessity for which is a disgrace to us as a civilised community—has brought some of the worst cases to light, but the fringe of the subject has merely been touched. Many children are being dragged up under the worst possible conditions. i Born n a noisome slum, amid dirt and smells, where fresh air and light penetrate under difficulties, many of the poorest children are handicapped in the battle of life from the start. When to this is added an in- difference on the part of parents respecting the up- bringing of their offspring, the marvel is that the infantile death rate is not very much higher. Let anyone who doubts the existence of such conditions in Warrington take a walk this evening to certain parts of the old town. Here he will find slums of a very bad type. It is hardly necessary to point them out to any who know the town. In the direction of Town's End we have Silver-square Haydock-street, and the contiguous streets all present- ing unwholesome surroundings. These hotbeds of disease and depravity are not confined to any particu- lar district. Slums of a like type are to be found on Cockhedge, at Dial-court, Morris'-court, Ship-yard, Dolman's-lane, and the courts behind Knutsford-road. The denizens of these dismal alleys and courts are almost as repulsive as their surroundings. Of drunken and profligate habits they give the police endlesstrouble and appear in the dock at the police court with great regularity. Their bodies smell abominably as anyone can testify who has experienced a whiff in the police or CountyoCourts. Equally nauseating is the smell of children from these slums owing to their dirty and , neglected condition. They move hither and thither among the community, and then we are surprised at the prevalence of disease! Ido not wish for one moment to advance the plea that Warrington is worse than other places. There are probably other towns in a worse plight. In Edinburgh, for instance, which prides itself on being the .nost beautiful city in the world, I have seen closes and insanitary areas near the historic Cannongate which would vie with any to be seen in Warrington. It is much easier to point out these causes of disease than to suggest remedies. But until our sanitary authority wakes up to its neglected responsibilities in i respect of insanitary areas, t is useless lamenting the high death-rate of the Borough. We require a strong man in the Council Chamhsr who will brush aside the subterfuges and dodges of owners of slum property, and insist upon prompt action being taken under the Housing of the Working Classes Act. There are many houses unfit for human habitation and no amount of tinkering and patching up can make them fit. Until they are removed the public health will continue to suffer, and we shall occupy the unenviable position of having a high death rate. The suggestion of Mr. R. T. Fairclough at the last meeting of the Education Committee, that a nurse should be appointed to undertake the medical super- vision of children attending the schools, deserves con- sideration. The members of the teaching profession already perform a useful work in detecting disease amongst school children but they cannot follow the . children further than the school door. It. requires someone who will see the child home, and if necessary, take steps for its proper treatment. The employment of a trained nurse or nurses for this work would perhaps prove costly but there is reason to believe that the cost would be a small matter in comparison with the great good which would follow in a diminution of disease amongst children. And what is to be done to solve the drink and dirt problem? Several agencies are at work but much remains to be accomplished before any visible impression can be made. It is reported of a mission- ary in South Africa that he once spoke of Keating's powder as being the greatest need of a certain tribe. In Warrington, sobriety and cleanliness seem to be our greatest social needs at the moment, and any improvement which can be effected in this direction will be reflected in the improved health of the town. J. H.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROPOSED CLOSING OF SCHOOLS. Meeting of the Health Committee.

At the meeting of the Health Committee of the Cor- poration on Wednesday evening the suggestion made by the Medical Officer of Health to the Education Committee that owing to the ' continued prevalence of infectious disease all the public elementary schools in the Borough. should be closed at once until the day already fixed for their re-opening after the midsummer holidays (August 15th) was under consideration. The School Management Committee considered the Medical Officer's suggestion at a meeting held on the Bth inst. and expressed the opinion that in view of their experi- ence of closing schools during the present school year, they were not satisfied that the suggested closure of the schools would have the desired effect. This deci- sion was confirmed at the meeting of the Education Committee held on Monday, evening, and in the speeches that were made it was stated that the Health Committee was shirking its responsibility in placing the onus of closing the schools on the Education Com- mittee. _ _

The Health Committee fully considered the matter on Wednesday evening and we uncierstand that the Chairman (Alderman Sutton) intends to make a state- ment at the next Council meeth,g setting forth the committee's position in the matter. So far as we can learn, the members of the Health Committee repudiate the suggestion of shirking their responsibility and I contend that as the Medical Officer of Health is the medical adviser of the Education committee, he was perfectly • justified in suggesting the advisa- bility of closing the schools to the Education Committee. The Health Committee, we are informed has no inten- tion of doing anything in regard to the closing of schools which would be contrary to the wishes of the Education Committee and in view of the improved health of the town during the past week it has been decided to take no further action in regard to the closing of schools.

The fielding of the Warringtonians was particularly keen, and the bowling of Lynes and Connor gave the visitors a warm time. Lynes captured six wickets at the small cost of 31 runs, and Connor took 4 for 27.

Up to Saturday, Earlestown had been particularly fortunate in away matches, having obtained six victories. On their own ground they had been beaten twice. Warrington are to be congratulated on administering so signal a defeat.

The second eleven experienced a trouncing at Earles- town, where they could only put together 8L The home team had replied with 130 for the loss of 8 wickets when the match closed. Alfred Thewlis and W. Hind made a good stand for Warrington's second wicket, the ex-professional knocking up 30 and Rind compiling 11. V. J. Reade was in grand form for Earlestown and made 52 not out.

There was an exciting match between Sankey Wire Mills and Irlam C.W.S., but Sankey triumphed by 3 runs. Taylor batted in capital style for the winners, his innings realizing 29. Llewellyn contributed an acceptable 14, whilst Intin (21) was the best batsman for Irlam.

Warrington econdary school entertained the War- grave eleven, and gained the victory by 35 runs. R. H. Albery was the only batsman to attain double figures, his total being 15. W. H. Houghton and J. Buckley did fine work with the ball, accounting for five wickets for 5 runs, and three wickets for 7 runs respectively.

Che3lii re Lines Engineers came •a cropper on their own ground, Latchford Baptists being the conquerors. The visitors ran up a total of 77, Lester being the highest scorer with 31. Latchford's efforts produced only 35, Duckworth being responsible for 24. Cliffe and Conway were the destructive agents. capturing five wiekets for one run, and two wickets for nothing r espeotively.

Latchford Baptists 2nd also scored a success, Lowton Independent having to admit defeat to the ext ent of 30 runs. H. Turner 15) and W. Southern (14 not out) batted well for the victors.

Matchers' gained a victory at the expense of St. Marys',s6 and 21 being the respective totals. Thompson batting for the home side, was in good form, his play being all to slip. Bate bowled splendidly, taking five wickets for seven runs. For the visitors Garry hit a splendid boundary, and he experienced hard luck in being caught for six runs. Dean was very sharp behind the wickets, saving a great many runs. Fletcher's lam told, had theadvantage as St. Marys' were not used to playing on matting, and in addition the grass was about two feet long.

St. Paul's were fortunate in beating Stratton and Appleton, the margin being 3. Mason (14) and Lawless (13) gave the best batting displays for the victors, and Fairhurst made a capital 11 nnt out for Stretton.

'Grappenhall sustained their first defeat of the season at the hands of Northwich. It was indeed hard lines for them to be beaten by two runs. W.Carter (16) and R. Gifford (15) batted splendidly for Grappenhall, and F. W. Bailey (16) ht,aded the Northwich score list. Grappenhall 2nd were defeated in no uncertain style by Arley.

My readers will be pleased to hear that J. Lynes, the Warrington professional, has received an invitation to assist Lancashire 2nd against Surrey 2nd at Kennington Oval. on Thursday and Friday, June 30th and July Ist.

Cycling.

Mouldsworth was the Cycling Club's destination on Saturday, and the run to this most picturesque part of Cheshire tempted nearly a score of members to turn out. The weather was fine, though at times rather gloomy, and the roads were in excellent condition. The outward journey was nlade via Frodshatn, Kingsley, and Manley,, and on arriving at Mouldsworth tea, was partaken of at the Railway Hotel. Th,f, ride home was accomplished by way of Kelsall, and Delarnere, and altogether the outing proved most enjoyable.

Tuesday evening's run was to Little Legh. The weather was very favourable, and there was quite a record turnout for this season. Twenty-four members and several friends assembled at the Leigh Arms, and an impromptu smoker was held, items beinp contributed by several members. A grand ride home in the moon- light brought to a close a very successful run.

That popular fixture "Ladies' day" is down for to-cLiy, and there should be a good turnout for the run to Lower Peover. On Tuesday the club run is to Lymm.

Nine members of the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Club journeyed to Rostherne on Saturday, and spent an enjoyable time at the picturesque village. The outward ride was made via Hoo Green, and the party returned via Lymm. There was again a turnout of nine members on Tuesday evening, when Cronton was the destination. To-day's run is to Pickmere.

Howard, who met with Erich a nasty spill on the racing track, is, I am glad to learn, progressing wonder- fully well towards complete recovery. He came out of the Liverpool Royal Infirmary last Saturday. His face is disfigured a little, and a gash in his shoulder has not quite healed up, but he is recovering very satisfactorily. He entered for Preston sports to-day but he does not feel fit to ride at present and will not turn out. He has, however, lost none of his confidence in the pastime and he only hopes that he may get back to his old form for the Football Sports at Wilderspool on August Monday.

Bowling.

Enthusiasts were again well catered for on Saturday, and some of the greens were thronged with spectators. The weather conditions were perfect, and bowlers have certainly reasoli to congratulate themselves upon having been favoured in this respect from the commencement of the season. Interest in the sport is fully maintained and both the works association and the local league are meeting with 9, succeps which must be very gratifying to the energeti officials. Saturday's matches were well contested, and in one there was a sensational , finish. There is every. promise of keen struggles for premier honours later in the season.

===== 0002601_19040625_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WORKS COMPETITION.

The large assembly on the delightfully-situated King and Queen green was evidence of the strong hold which the pastime has obtained on workingmen. A capital afternoon's sport was witnessed. Last year's champions —L. and N.-VV. Railway—tried conclusions with Pear- son and Knowles No. 2, but their display did not at all COMP up to their usual standard. They managed to obtain the victory by 18 points, but they will have to improve greatly if they hope to catry off the Finlayson cup again.

Mention must be made of several exciting games. P. Hill, the forge captain, defeated W. Gleave by a single. Gleave was the first to score, and had placed '6 to his credit before Hill got a chance. Hill then obtained a double, Gleave followed .with a single, and Hill got two more doubles. The scoring then ran :-6-8, 7-8, 7-10,

It is not easy to forecast the result of the first match but if the railway men do not show better fortn,Rylands's will in all probability be victorious. Pearson and Knowles should easily defeat Smith's, and Houghton's ought to win comfortably against Cockhedge.

The draw for the semi-final will take place on Monday evening.

THE LEAGUE.

How have the mighty fallen ! It must have been a great surprise to the redoubtable Cemetery men to be beaten by a team occupying such a lowly position as Fearnhead—and that by a 'single point. Of course the Fearnhead contingent had the advantage of playing on their own green, but even taking that into consideration the Cemetery club were, expected to give a much better account of themselves The finish was rather sensational, for Fearnhead were 18 points in arrears when the last two games com- menced. Joseph Weston and J. Roberts beat their opponents by 15-3 and 15-8 respectively, thus giving Fearnhead the victory by one point. The most keenly fought game was between H. Rowles and R. Rothwell, Bothwell held the upper hand for a long time, but his opponent gained upon him, and at the twenty-second end the totals stood at 1444. Rothwell then obtained the winning single. R. Mercer defeated W. Hill by 15-12. At one time Hill was leading by 11-7, but the subsequent scoring was :-8-11, 9-11, 10-11, 11-11, 13-11,13-12, 15-12.

The only other match was between Sankey Chapel House and Bull's Head, and here' too, a powerful organization had to admit defeat. Sankey played very creditably, and at the close led by 19 points. There was a good contest between W. Ellison and R. Fairbrother. At the sixteenth end matters haol reached an interesting stage, each contestant having scored 10. Then a double fell to Ellison, but Fair- brother obtained a single and a double in succession which placed him ahead by 13-12. Then each player had a!single, and the score stood at 14-13 for Fairbrother. Ellison delivered his woods with such judgment as to obtain a double, and the victory was his.

J. Worsley made a good stand against W. Unsworth, who, however, was able to win by 15-13. At the eighteenth end the score was 12-12, and the match pro- ceeded 13-12, 13-13, 14-13, 15-13.

OTHER MATCHES.

The Liberals and Conservatives hada friendly engage- ment on Monday, and the Liberals were victorious by 86 points. There were one or two close games, J. Parr defeating J. Horrabin by 13-12, and J. E. Warren (Liberal captain) beating J. Taylor (Conservative captain) by 13-11.

Saracen's Head team went to Walton on Saturday, and ran out easy winners by 85.

There will be some interesting games on the Cemetery Hotel green on Monday evening, when the semi-final for the Allison dap and other prizes presented for com- petition to the members of the Cemetery Club, will be decided. Golf.

Congratulations to Dr. Peacocke ! It is no mean honour to carry off the Balfour Golf Cup,and the mem- bers of the new Warrington Club, with which Dr. Pea- cocke is associated, will be especially gratified to hear of his sdccess. Dr. Peacocke has had a most successful career as a golfer, and it is mainly due to his enthusiasm for the game and constant practice, without which no one can attain proficiency. He first played ten years ago in connection with the Mersey Golf Club, and 119 has been a member of the Timperley combina- tion for about six years. He has won the captain's medal at Penketh no fewer than three times, and the captain's prize at Timperley two years ago,with a gross scote of 81. His latest victory is of course the greatest triumph of all.

The "Manchester Guardian," commenting on the Timperley success, says :—`• At Timperley on Saturday was decided what may be termed, without a very severe strain on language, our local golf championship. One of the salient points of the Balfour Cup competition is that while it provides all the excitement of a thorough going championship it has non 9of its finality. Its pro- moters some seven years ago rightly judged that to give it the essence of a championship was to make it a pre- tentious parochial thing, to show a plentiful lack of a saving sense of proportion. 'Let a championship, connote championship' said they; as for us we will provide a golf meeting which by its equal opportunity will attract the player and en- courage the game.' That they persuade& something like a hundred and , twenty—golfers and others—to start fair ' on Saturday was a gratifying achieement.

There is, it may fairly be suggested, more of the one touch of nature here than in the really big events. In the latter you have to show yourself better than a hun- dred or so others—a presumptuous endeavour at the best; in the Balfour you have to show yourself better than you pertonally are supposed to be. The handicap broadly and professedly reflects public opinion; there is here the temptation to upset accepted standards. Dr. Peacocke has yielded to it successfully. The conclusion is not merely that he holds the Balfour Cup for a year, but that he showed himself on Saturday even a finer golfer than had been supposed. It would be interesting to learn how far he attributed his success to any particular instrument. Mr. Travis a fortnight ago modestly hid has greatness behind his mallet-headed putter, and one of the successful pair in the Houldsworth on Wednesday seemed inclined to the same shyness. There were several of these tools about on Wednesday and Saturday. Their number deprives Dr. Peacocke of the possibility of this defence of diffi- dence, for there were players on Saturday usi nt the magic weapon who did not win the Balfour Cup. With- out further rebutting evidence the man in the street will be forced to the conclusion thatDr..Peacocke won by his golf." The cup is now on view an the Sankey- street window of Mr. Birtles' studio. It bears the fol- lowing inscription :—"The Balfour Challenge Cup. Presented to the golfers of Manchester by the Right Hon. A. J. Balfour. April, 1897."

The Cup has been won in former years as follows :- 1897.—At Timperley.—S. W. Gillett, Bowdon, 87, scratch-87. , 1898.—At Worsley.—A. G. Hogg Bowdon, 82,sc ratch —B2 1899.—At Trafford Park.—W. G. Clegg, I3owdon, 79, scratc h-79. 1900.—At rimperley.—H. P. Wynne, Entwistle, 80 less 7-75. 1901.—At Withington.—A. Ramm, Timperley (handicap 1), won at te eleventh hole (45 strokes to 54), after a tie with R. 0. J. Dallmeyer, Bowdon (pins 1). 1902.—At Worsley.—H. Naylor:Ashton (88, less 4_84), after a tie with T. C. Mellor, Cheadle (scratch). 1903.—At Trafford Park.—F. Marsh, Didsbury, 76, less 2-74.

PENKETH. A LOCAL SUCCESS.—The friends of Miss Daisy Snoddy of Penketh will be pleased to learn of her successes in winning a First Class in the Examination of the London, City and Guilds Institute in dressmaking, and also a First Class Advanced of the Lancashire and Cheshire i Institute, after attending a course of lectures at the Municipal School of Technology Manchester. The former examination extended over three days. Miss Snoddy was previously a student of the Warrington Mtuaicipal classes. YOUTH'S GALLANT ItIiSCUE. On Tuesday night Harry Walker, aged ten, was bath- ing in the river Weaver at Fr6dshamßridge, with other boys none of whom could swim, when he got out of his depth and sank three times. Harry Heselton, aged twelve, who was on the bank fully dressed, pluckily jumped in, swam to the middle of the stream, caught hold of the drowning boy's neck, and swam back to the bank. FATAL OVER-STUDY. Reginald Mussell, a young schoolmaster, recently ap- pointed to a good position at Colchester, was found dead on Wednesday with his throat cut. He had become depressed through oTer-study.

Gornall, of Warrington A.C., was first in the half-mile novice bicycle race at Crewe Athletic sports.' A. Warren, of Sankey Harriers A.C., was second in the' half mile flat race at Blackburn; and G. Flint, of Thel- wall A.C. was third in the quarter-mile handicap at Huddersfield. -- -- Swimming. Mr. William A. Starkey, a Birmingham business Iflac. has died as the result of a scratch received while swim- ming. The scratch was caused by another swimmer,but it was not until a few days after that he thought any raore about it. The wound began to take bad ways,and after a few days' treatment in the house it was found that the poison had gone too far, and that amputation of the limb would be useless. Water Polo. Teams representing Lancashire and Cheshire opposed: each other in the Hyde Corporation Baths on Taesdal evening, for the County Championship. The Cestrians started well, and were soon testing the visiting defence - Play was very even for a time, but Gallagher was the first to score for the Lancastrians. Swift (Wigan) vree ordered out of the bath for ungentlemanly conduct. Shaw scored for Cheshire, and the interval arrived With the scores even. In the second half three players were' ordered out of the water for ungentlemanly conduct- Davis scored second goal for Lancashire, and the holders won the game by two goals to one. --- Great interest centered in Warrington's re-played tie. with Everton in the Lancashire County Competition oa Thursday evening, and there was a crowded attendance' at the Margaret-street Baths, Liverpool. Warringtol' defended the deep end, and from the start the home' team pressed, but found Wood —7,in in grand form at goal. A passing movement initiated by J. Formby:, ended in L. Hayes scoring. Just prior to the interval' A. BOscow was ordered out for walking. Despite their being a man short, no further score resulted, and half- time arrived with Evertor' 1. Warrington 0. Resuming; the visitors attacked vigorously, and A. Ashton sooreu a goal. For Everton, J. Formby replied with two more. Play now became exciting, each team attacking in tart At length C. Ashton scored from near half-way, but td the surprise of all, the referee, Mr. Finegan, disallowe the point. This decision did not suit the Wirepuller*. and as a mark of protest, they left the water without finishing the game. The final score was therefore' Everton 3, Warrington 1. - --- A meeting will be held on Monday evening to decide whether to lodge a protest against the referee's rulieg or to submit to the decision. The second team visited Birkenhead on Tuesday eve° ing, and triumphed by six goals to two.
